\end{tabular}
\end{center}
($T_{\mathrm{eff}}=\Phi'/\pi$; the median is the height, in horizons, below which half of the floor sits. At $a=1.5$ the Hellmann--Feynman contraction failed, two inverse-iteration steps at $800$ bits leaving the $K=230$ eigenvector short of the $10^{-90}$ relative accuracy the contraction needs, and $\Phi'=491.1$ is the central difference of the $K=230$ floor between $1.495$ and $1.505$ from Computation~\ref{comp:grid}; the tail law returns it, $T\vartheta/\Tst=1.233,\,1.230$ at $7$ and $10$ horizons against $\Phi'/(\pi\Tst)=1.239$. The $\lambda_K$ of the $a=1.25$ row of Computation~\ref{comp:identity} was corrected on this occasion to $3.4161\times10^{-51}$, the value the zeros return; its $\Phi'/\Tst$ was already the correct one.)
The tail law holds on the $K$-mode minimizers to $1$--$3\%$ from five horizons on. Below that, the plunge region $[\Tst,3\Tst]$ carries more than the law, $T\vartheta/\Tst$ reaching $1.4$--$1.5$ at $1.5\Tst$; the median height of the floor's mass is $2.39,\,2.32,\,2.65,\,2.67,\,2.74$ horizons at $a=0.6,\,0.8,\,1.0,\,1.25,\,1.5$ (and $1.8,\,2.0,\,2.4$ on the FEM minimizers at $0.4,\,0.45,\,0.5$), rising with $a$ and, at the last point, at $e=2.72$ The curves $\vartheta(T/\Tst)$ at $a=0.6$ and $0.8$ coincide to $0.01$ at every point of the grid, those at $0.4$, $0.45$, $0.5$ approach them from below as $a$ grows, and those at $1.0$, $1.25$ and $1.5$ lie above them in the plunge region ($\vartheta(2\Tst)=0.81,\,0.78,\,0.86$ against $0.59$) and rejoin them in the tail. Measured in units of $T_{\mathrm{eff}}$ rather than $\Tst$, that is as $T\vartheta(T)/T_{\mathrm{eff}}$, all five agree to $5\%$ from $2.5$ horizons on ($1.10$--$1.14$ at $2.5$, $1.05$--$1.18$ at $3$, $1.01$--$1.07$ at $5$, $0.99$--$1.01$ at $10$), across floors spanning $86$ orders of magnitude
\end{computation}

\begin{conjecture}[tail law]\label{conj:tail}
Assume RH. For every $a>0$ that is not an entry, with $f$ the minimizer at support $a$ and $\lambda'$ the derivative of the floor,
\[
\lim_{T\to\infty}\;T\sum_{|\gamma|>T}|F(\gamma)|^2\;=\;-\frac{\lambda'(a)}{\pi}\,;\qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad \Phi'(a)=\pi\,T_{\mathrm{eff}}(a),\quad T_{\mathrm{eff}}(a)=\lim_{T\to\infty}T\vartheta(T).
\]
Under (H$_\infty$) this is a theorem for $a_2<a<a_3$ (Corollary~\ref{cor:full}), at every support below $a_\infty=0.843$ where the echo set is finite (Corollary~\ref{cor:tree}), and for the archimedean form at every support (Corollary~\ref{cor:primefree}).
\end{conjecture}

The heuristic behind it is the edge law: a shape with $f(a-\delta)\sim C(\log(a/\delta))^{-1/2}$ has $|F(t)|^2\approx4C^2\cos^2(ta)/(t^2\log t)$ for large $t$, the zeros have density $\log(t/2\pi)/2\pi$, and the product integrated from $T$ gives $C^2/(\pi T)$, which is $-\lambda'/(2\pi T)$ by Conjecture~\ref{conj:hadamard}. The asymptotic expansion carries a ratio of logarithms, $\log(T/2\pi)/(\log(aT)+\beta)$, that would be $1.1$ to $1.4$ at the heights of Computation~\ref{comp:tail} and that the data do not show; the law is sharper than its derivation, which suggests a sum rule behind it. In the form $\Phi'=\pi T_{\mathrm{eff}}$ the two conjectures of Section~\ref{sec:conj} read: Conjecture~\ref{conj:A} is $T_{\mathrm{eff}}(a)\le(c/\pi)\Tst(a)$, the effective height of the zeros that carry the floor is a bounded multiple of the horizon; Conjecture~\ref{conj:B} is $T_{\mathrm{eff}}/\Tst\to c_0/\pi$, and the measured $c_0$ between $3.2$ and $3.9$ is $T_{\mathrm{eff}}$ between $1.0$ and $1.24$ horizons.

The limit in Conjecture~\ref{conj:tail} is a theorem once the edge law is assumed with two derivatives.

\begin{proposition}[edge law implies tail law]\label{prop:tail}
Assume RH. Let $f$ be real, odd, supported in $[-a,a]$ and $C^2$ on $(-a,a)$, and suppose that for some $C>0$, $\beta\in\R$ and $\delta_0>0$,
\[
f(a-\delta)=C\big(\log(a/\delta)+\beta\big)^{-1/2}+\psi(\delta),\qquad 0<\delta<\delta_0,\qquad \psi^{(k)}(\delta)=o\big(\delta^{-k}\log^{-3/2}(1/\delta)\big)\ \ (k=0,1,2)\ \text{as }\delta\to0^+ .
\]
Then
\[
\lim_{T\to\infty}\;T\sum_{|\gamma|>T}|F(\gamma)|^2\;=\;\frac{2C^2}{\pi}.
\]
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
\emph{Step 1: the transform.} Put $L(\delta)=\log(a/\delta)+\beta$, $\varphi(\delta)=CL(\delta)^{-1/2}$, so $\varphi'(\delta)=\tfrac C2L^{-3/2}\delta^{-1}>0$ and $|\varphi''(\delta)|\le C'L^{-3/2}\delta^{-2}$ on $(0,\delta_0)$. Let $\chi$ be smooth, $1$ on $[0,\delta_0/2]$ and $0$ beyond $\delta_0$; put $f_+(x)=(\varphi+\psi)(a-x)\chi(a-x)$ near the right edge, $f_-(x)=-f_+(-x)$, and $f_0=f-f_+-f_-$, which is $C^2$ with compact support in $(-a,a)$, so $F_0(t)=O(t^{-2})$. With $u=(\varphi+\psi)\chi$, $u(0^+)=u(\delta_0)=0$, one integration by parts gives $F_+(t)=e^{ita}\int_0^{\delta_0}u(\delta)e^{-it\delta}d\delta=e^{ita}(it)^{-1}\int_0^{\delta_0}u'(\delta)e^{-it\delta}d\delta$. In $u'=\varphi'\chi+\psi'\chi+(\varphi+\psi)\chi'$ the last term is smooth and supported away from $0$, contributing $O(1/t)$. For $J(t)=\int_0^{\delta_0}\varphi'\chi e^{-it\delta}d\delta$ split at $\delta=1/t$: on $[0,1/t]$, $e^{-it\delta}=1+O(t\delta)$ gives $\varphi(1/t)+O\big(t\int_0^{1/t}\varphi'\delta\,d\delta\big)=\varphi(1/t)+O(L(1/t)^{-3/2})$; on $[1/t,\delta_0]$ one more integration by parts gives $O(\varphi'(1/t)/t)+O\big(t^{-1}\int_{1/t}^{\delta_0}|(\varphi'\chi)'|\big)=O(L(1/t)^{-3/2})$. Hence $J(t)=CL(1/t)^{-1/2}(1+O(1/\log t))$. The same splitting applied to $\int\psi'\chi e^{-it\delta}$, with $\int_0^{1/t}|\psi'|=o(\log^{-1/2}t)$ and the hypothesis on $\psi''$, gives $o(\log^{-1/2}t)$. Therefore $F_+(t)=e^{ita}(it)^{-1}CL(1/t)^{-1/2}(1+O(1/\log t))$, and since $f$ is real and odd, $F_-(t)=-\overline{F_+(t)}=e^{-ita}(it)^{-1}CL(1/t)^{-1/2}(1+O(1/\log t))$. So
\[
F(t)=\frac{2C\cos(ta)}{it\sqrt{L(1/t)}}\big(1+O(1/\log t)\big)+O(t^{-2}),\qquad |F(t)|^2=\frac{4C^2\cos^2(ta)}{t^2L(1/t)}\big(1+O(1/\log t)\big)+O\big(t^{-3}\big).
\]
\emph{Step 2: the sum over the zeros.} Under RH the zeros are real and $N(t)=\frac t{2\pi}\log\frac t{2\pi e}+\frac78+S(t)+R(t)$ with $R$ smooth, $R=O(1/t)$, $R'=O(t^{-2})$, and, by Littlewood \cite[Ch.\ XIV, \S14.13]{titchmarsh}, $S(t)=O(\log t/\log\log t)$ and $S_1(t)=\int_0^tS=O(\log t/(\log\log t)^2)$. Put $w(t)=t^{-2}L(1/t)^{-1}$, $\rho(t)=\frac1{2\pi}\log\frac t{2\pi}$, so $\rho/L(1/t)\cdot t^2w=1+O(1/\log t)$. Since $\sum_{|\gamma|>T}=2\sum_{\gamma>T}$ and $4C^2\cos^2(ta)w=2C^2w(1+\cos2ta)$, the claim is $\sum_{\gamma>T}w(\gamma)=\frac1{2\pi T}(1+o(1))$, $\sum_{\gamma>T}\cos(2\gamma a)w(\gamma)=o(1/T)$, and $\sum_{\gamma>T}\big(w(\gamma)/\log\gamma+\gamma^{-3}\big)=o(1/T)$. For a positive decreasing $g$, $\sum_{\gamma>T}g(\gamma)=\int_T^\infty g\,dN=\int_T^\infty g\rho\,dt-g(T)S(T)-\int_T^\infty Sg'\,dt+\int_T^\infty gR'\,dt$, and with $|S|\ll\log t$ the last three terms are $O(g(T)\log T+\int_T^\infty|g'|\log t+\int_T^\infty g/t^2)$: for $g=w$ this is $O(\log T/T^2)=o(1/T)$ while $\int_T^\infty w\rho=\frac1{2\pi T}(1+O(1/\log T))$; for $g=w/\log t$ and $g=t^{-3}$ everything is $o(1/T)$. For the oscillatory sum put $G(t)=\cos(2ta)w(t)$: $\int_T^\infty G\rho\,dt=O(w(T)\rho(T)/a)=o(1/T)$ by one integration by parts against $\sin(2ta)/2a$, $\int_T^\infty GR'=o(1/T)$, and
\[
\int_T^\infty G\,dS=-G(T)S(T)+G'(T)S_1(T)+\int_T^\infty S_1G''\,dt,
\]
where $|G(T)S(T)|=O(\log T\,w(T))$, $|G'(T)S_1(T)|=O(a\log T\,w(T))$, and $|G''|\le4a^2w+O(w/t)$ gives $\int_T^\infty|S_1G''|=O\big(a^2\int_T^\infty\frac{\log t}{(\log\log t)^2t^2L(1/t)}dt\big)=O\big(\frac{a^2}{T(\log\log T)^2}\big)$, all $o(1/T)$. Hence $\sum_{|\gamma|>T}|F(\gamma)|^2=4C^2\cdot\frac1{2\pi T}(1+o(1))=\frac{2C^2}{\pi T}(1+o(1))$.
\end{proof}

With Conjecture~\ref{conj:hadamard}, $-\lambda'=2C^2$, the proposition is Conjecture~\ref{conj:tail}; its hypothesis is the edge law of Theorem~\ref{thm:edge} strengthened to an asymptotic equality with two derivatives, which is what Computation~\ref{comp:edge} measures and (H$_\infty$) does not give; for the prime-free form, Proposition~\ref{prop:profile} derives that strengthening, with its constants, from (H$_\infty$) and the Wiener--Hopf representation. RH enters twice, to read $Q$ as the sum over real zeros and through Littlewood's bound on $S_1$; without the latter the sum over the zeros is still $O(1/T)$, only the constant is lost. The $O(1/\log T)$ in the proof is the correction seen in the density integral of Computation~\ref{comp:tail}; that the zero sums show none of it at one percent is the sum rule still to be found. The Euler--Lagrange equation gives an exact identity for the tail at every height, in which the logarithms cancel.

\begin{proposition}[tail identity]\label{prop:tailid}
Let $f$ be a minimizer at support $a$ and let $K$ be the operator with symbol $\Psi$, so that $Q(f)=\ip{f}{Kf}-2P(f)^2$ and $f$ satisfies $Kf=\lambda f+2P(f)\,s$ on $(-a,a)$ with $s(y)=\sinh(y/2)\mathbf 1_{[-a,a]}(y)$. Put $g_{\mathrm{out}}=Kf\cdot\mathbf 1_{|y|>a}$, the force outside the window, and let $f_T$ be the low-pass of $f$ at height $T$, $\widehat{f_T}=F\mathbf 1_{|t|\le T}$. Then for every $T>0$
\[
\frac1{2\pi}\int_{|t|>T}|F(t)|^2\big(\Psi(t)-\lambda\big)\,dt\;=\;\frac{2P(f)}{2\pi}\int_{|t|>T}\overline{F(t)}\,\Sigma(t)\,dt\;-\;\int_{|y|>a}f_T(y)\,g_{\mathrm{out}}(y)\,dy,\qquad \Sigma=\hat s .
\]
Explicitly, for $|y|>a$, $g_{\mathrm{out}}(y)=-\int J_\Gamma(y-z)f(z)\,dz-\sum_n\Lambda(n)n^{-1/2}\big(f(y-\log n)+f(y+\log n)\big)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
On the whole line $Kf=\lambda f+2Ps+g_{\mathrm{out}}$, since the window equation holds inside and $g_{\mathrm{out}}$ is by definition the value of $Kf$ outside. In Fourier form, $(\Psi-\lambda)F=2P\Sigma+G$ with $G=\widehat{g_{\mathrm{out}}}$. Multiply by $\overline F\mathbf 1_{|t|>T}$ and integrate: the term with $G$ is $\frac1{2\pi}\int_{|t|>T}\overline FG=\ip{f-f_T}{g_{\mathrm{out}}}$ by Parseval, and $\ip f{g_{\mathrm{out}}}=0$ because the supports are disjoint. The formula for $g_{\mathrm{out}}$ is the kernel of $K$ off the diagonal: from \eqref{eq:Winf}, $(K_\infty f)(y)=c_0f(y)+\int_0^\infty\big[2f(y)e^{-2x}-(f(y-x)+f(y+x))e^{-x/2}\big](1-e^{-2x})^{-1}dx$, which for $f(y)=0$ is $-\int J_\Gamma(y-z)f(z)\,dz$, and the prime part of $Q$ is the operator $-\sum_n\Lambda(n)n^{-1/2}(\tau_{\log n}+\tau_{-\log n})$. Both sides converge: $g_{\mathrm{out}}$ is smooth away from $\pm a$, decays exponentially, and by the edge law grows like $-C(\log(1/\delta))^{1/2}$ at distance $\delta$ outside the edge, while $f_T$ is bounded and $O(1/|y|)$.
\end{proof}

The identity is the mechanism of the tail law. Just outside the edge, $g_{\mathrm{out}}(a+\delta)=-C\,L(\delta)^{1/2}(1+o(1))$ with $L(\delta)=\log(a/\delta)+\beta$ (the kernel $1/(2|s|)$ acting on the edge profile $CL^{-1/2}$), and the low-pass of $f$ leaks outside the edge as $f_T(a+\delta)=C\,L(1/T)^{-1/2}\,\pi^{-1}\big(\tfrac\pi2-\operatorname{Si}(T\delta)\big)(1+o(1))$; their overlap is $2C^2/(\pi T)$ times $\int_0^\infty(\tfrac\pi2-\operatorname{Si})=1$, with $L^{-1/2}L^{1/2}=1$: no logarithm, and it is the tail law, since the left side is $\int_{|t|>T}|F|^2\rho\,(1+o(1))$. The remaining pieces, the overlap with the smooth part of $g_{\mathrm{out}}$ (the regular part of the kernel, the other edge, the reflected primes) and the polar integral, are each of order $C\,L(1/T)^{-1/2}/T$, larger than $C^2/T$ when $C$ is small, and must cancel.

\begin{computation}[the tail identity on the FEM minimizers]\label{comp:tailid}
\texttt{rh\_sum\_rule.py}, on the edge-FEM minimizers with primes at $a=0.5$ and $0.45$ (Computation~\ref{comp:hadamard}; the prime $2$ is the only one in the window). The left side is the frequency integral of the closed-form transform against $\Psi-\lambda$ from $T$ to $400\,\Tst$, with the tail beyond by Computation~\ref{comp:tail}; $g_{\mathrm{out}}$ is the archimedean kernel applied to the piecewise-linear $f$, the $1/(2s)$ part of $J_\Gamma$ integrated exactly on every segment and the smooth remainder by Gauss--Legendre, plus the reflected prime; $f_T$ is the inverse sine transform of the closed-form $F$ on $[0,T]$. At $a=0.5$, $T/\Tst=5,10,20$: right side over left side $1.000,\,0.990,\,0.980$; at $a=0.45$, $T/\Tst=5,20$: $1.011,\,0.989$. Beyond twenty horizons the grid in $y$ no longer resolves the oscillation of the leak, whose period is $2\pi/T$, and the ratio drifts to $0.94$ and $0.91$ at $50$ and $100$ horizons at $a=0.5$; the left side itself equals the law of Computation~\ref{comp:tail} to $0.1\%$ there. The polar piece is $0.30,\,0.30,\,0.28$ of the left side at $a=0.5$, so it is not small. The overlap of $f_T$ with the edge-law force $-C\,L(\delta)^{1/2}$ alone, on $\delta<a/10$ with the $C,\beta$ of Computation~\ref{comp:hadamard}, is $0.93,\,1.09,\,0.98$ of the law $-\lambda'/(\pi T)$ at $a=0.5$ and $1.07,\,1.01$ at $a=0.45$, and the rest of the overlap plus the polar piece is $+0.09,\,-0.10,\,-0.01$ and $-0.04,\,-0.02$ of the law: the edge overlap is the law and the two order-$CL^{-1/2}/T$ pieces cancel, each to about ten percent. What remains of the sum rule is analytic: the overlap of the leak with $-CL^{1/2}$ as an asymptotic expansion in $1/L(1/T)$ (the leading term has no logarithm; the first correction is not yet derived and is at most ten percent at two units of $L$), and the cancellation of the smooth overlap against the polar piece, which is a statement about the reflected primes and the regular part of the kernel at the edge.
\end{computation}

The first of these is settled by a symmetry.

\begin{lemma}[the edge overlap has no first-order logarithmic correction]\label{lem:overlap}
Let $L(\delta)=\log(a/\delta)+\beta$, $\varphi(\delta)=CL(\delta)^{-1/2}$ the edge profile and $\gamma_{\mathrm{e}}(\delta)=-CL(\delta)^{1/2}$ the edge force, both on $0<\delta<\delta_0$, and let the leak of $\varphi$ through the sharp low-pass at height $T$ be $\ell_T(\delta)=\int_0^{\delta_0}\varphi(u)\,\frac{\sin(T(\delta+u))}{\pi(\delta+u)}\,du$. Then, with $L_T=L(1/T)$,
\[
-2\int_0^{\delta_0}\ell_T(\delta)\,\gamma_{\mathrm{e}}(\delta)\,d\delta\;=\;\frac{2C^2}{\pi T}\,J(L_T),\qquad J(L)=\int_0^\infty\!\!\int_0^\infty\Big[\frac{L+\log(1/v)}{L+\log(1/s)}\Big]^{1/2}\frac{\sin(s+v)}{s+v}\,ds\,dv\;=\;1+O(L^{-2}),
\]
the integrals taken in the Abel sense and cut where the model ends. The term of order $1/L$ vanishes: the kernel $\sin(s+v)/(s+v)$ is symmetric in $(s,v)$ while $\log\big[(L+\log(1/v))/(L+\log(1/s))\big]$ is antisymmetric, so only even powers of it survive, and the leading one is $\tfrac18\log^2(s/v)/L^2$, which integrates to $\pi^2/(24L^2)$ against $\int_0^1\log^2\frac{1-\sigma}\sigma\,d\sigma=\pi^2/3$ and $\int_0^\infty\sin w\,dw=1$ after the substitution $s=w\sigma$, $v=w(1-\sigma)$. Numerically $J(L)=1.020,\,1.012,\,1.008,\,1.005,\,1.005,\,1.003$ for $L=3,\dots,8$ (\texttt{rh\_sum\_rule.py}); the series $1+\pi^2/(24L^2)-1.898/L^3+\dots$ is asymptotic and useful only from $L\approx7$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Substitute $u=s/T$, $\delta=v/T$ in the double integral, so that $\varphi(s/T)\gamma_{\mathrm e}(v/T)=-C^2\,[L_T+\log(1/v)]^{1/2}[L_T+\log(1/s)]^{-1/2}$ and the kernel becomes $T\sin(s+v)/(\pi(s+v))$ with $du\,d\delta=T^{-2}ds\,dv$. Write the ratio as $\exp(u)$ with $u=\tfrac12[\log(1+x_v)-\log(1+x_s)]$, $x=\log(1/\cdot)/L_T$; $u$ changes sign under $s\leftrightarrow v$ and the kernel does not, so $\int\int e^u\,k=\int\int\cosh(u)\,k=\int\int(1+u^2/2+\dots)k$. With $s=w\sigma$, $v=w(1-\sigma)$, $ds\,dv=w\,dw\,d\sigma$ and $u=\tfrac12\log\frac{1-\sigma}\sigma\,L_T^{-1}+O(L_T^{-2})$ is independent of $w$ at leading order, which gives the constant and the second-order term.
\end{proof}

So the edge overlap is the tail law with a correction of order $L(1/T)^{-2}$, one to two percent at the heights of Computation~\ref{comp:tail}, which is what the zero sums show. What remains of the sum rule is the cancellation of the smooth overlap against the polar piece. At leading order both are $2CL_T^{-1/2}/(\pi T)$ times a constant: the polar piece is $2P\ip{f}{s_{>T}}$ with the high-pass of $s=\sinh(y/2)\mathbf 1_{[-a,a]}$ concentrated in the Gibbs layer of width $1/T$ at the edges, giving $4P\sinh(a/2)\,CL_T^{-1/2}/(\pi T)$, and the smooth overlap is $-2g_{\mathrm{reg}}(a)\,CL_T^{-1/2}/(\pi T)$ with $g_{\mathrm{reg}}(a)$ the regular part of $g_{\mathrm{out}}$ at the edge. They cancel if and only if $g_{\mathrm{reg}}(a)=2P\sinh(a/2)$, which is the value of $Kf$ just inside the edge by the eigen-equation: the sum rule reduces to the statement that the regular part of $Kf$ is continuous across the edge, the singularity $\mp CL^{1/2}$ being carried entirely by the outside. Computation~\ref{comp:tailid} supports it to about twenty percent of $g_{\mathrm{reg}}(a)$. It is a local statement, and it holds.

\begin{lemma}[the regular part of $Kf$ is continuous across the edge]\label{lem:cont}
Let $f$ satisfy the hypothesis of Proposition~\ref{prop:tail} and put $\varphi(\delta)=CL(\delta)^{-1/2}$. Then
\[
\lim_{\delta\to0^+}\Big[(K_\infty f)(a-\delta)-(K_\infty f)(a+\delta)-CL(\delta)^{1/2}\Big]=0 .
\]
Consequently, for the minimizer, $g_{\mathrm{reg}}(a):=\lim_{\delta\to0^+}\big[g_{\mathrm{out}}(a+\delta)+CL(\delta)^{1/2}\big]=(Kf)(a^-)=2P(f)\sinh(a/2)$, and the function $h=2P(f)s+g_{\mathrm{reg}}$ is continuous at $\pm a$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write $k(x)=e^{-x/2}/(1-e^{-2x})=\frac1{2x}+\frac14+O(x)$ and $m(x)=e^{-2x}/(1-e^{-2x})=\frac1{2x}-\frac12+O(x)$, so that by \eqref{eq:Winf} $(K_\infty f)(y)=c_0f(y)+\int_0^\infty\big[2f(y)m(x)-(f(y-x)+f(y+x))k(x)\big]dx$. Outside, $f(a+\delta)=0$ gives $(K_\infty f)(a+\delta)=-\int_0^{2a}f(a-u)\,k(u+\delta)\,du$. Inside, with $f(a-\delta+x)=\varphi(\delta-x)\mathbf 1_{x<\delta}$, split the integral at $x=\delta$: on $[0,\delta]$ it is $\int_0^\delta\big[2\varphi(\delta)(m-k)+(2\varphi(\delta)-\varphi(\delta+x)-\varphi(\delta-x))k\big]dx=O(\delta\varphi(\delta))+O\big(\int_0^\delta x^2|\varphi''|k\big)=o(1)$, since $|\varphi''|\le C'L^{-3/2}\delta^{-2}$; on $[\delta,\infty)$ it is $2\varphi(\delta)\int_\delta^\infty m-\int_\delta^\infty\varphi(\delta+x)k(x)\,dx=\varphi(\delta)\log\frac1{1-e^{-2\delta}}-\int_{2\delta}^{2a}f(a-u)\,k(u-\delta)\,du$. Hence
\[
(K_\infty f)(a-\delta)-(K_\infty f)(a+\delta)-CL(\delta)^{1/2}=\Big[\varphi(\delta)\log\tfrac1{2\delta}-CL(\delta)^{1/2}\Big]+\int_0^{2a}f(a-u)\big[k(u+\delta)-k(u-\delta)\mathbf 1_{u>2\delta}\big]du+o(1).
\]
The bracket is $CL(\delta)^{-1/2}\big[\log\tfrac1{2\delta}-L(\delta)\big]=-C(\beta+\log2a)L(\delta)^{-1/2}\to0$. In the integral, the part over $u<2\delta$ is $O(\varphi(2\delta))$, and over $u>2\delta$ one has $k(u+\delta)-k(u-\delta)=-\delta/(u^2-\delta^2)+O(\delta)$, whose contribution is $O\big(\delta\int_{2\delta}\varphi(u)u^{-2}du\big)+O(\delta)=O(\varphi(2\delta))+O(\delta)\to0$. The terms with $\psi$ in place of $\varphi$ are integrable against $1/u$ by the hypothesis on $\psi$ and contribute the same continuous function on both sides. For the consequence, $g_{\mathrm{out}}=K_\infty f+K_Pf$ outside and $K_Pf$ is continuous across the edge ($f$ is continuous), so $g_{\mathrm{reg}}(a)=(K_\infty f)(a^-)+(K_Pf)(a)=(Kf)(a^-)=\lambda f(a)+2P\sinh(a/2)$, and $f(a)=0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[the sum rule]\label{cor:sumrule}
Under the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{prop:tail}, for the minimizer,
\[
\frac1{2\pi}\int_{|t|>T}|F|^2(\Psi-\lambda)\,dt\;=\;\frac{2C^2}{\pi T}\Big(1+O\big(L(1/T)^{-1}\big)\Big),
\]
the edge overlap contributing $\frac{2C^2}{\pi T}(1+O(L^{-2}))$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:overlap} and the polar piece plus the smooth overlap contributing $O(C^2/(TL))$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
In the identity of Proposition~\ref{prop:tailid} write $g_{\mathrm{out}}=-CL^{1/2}\mathbf 1_{\mathrm{edge}}+g_{\mathrm{reg}}$. The polar piece is $2P\ip f{s_{>T}}$ and the smooth overlap is $-\ip{f_T}{g_{\mathrm{reg}}}=-\ip f{g_{\mathrm{reg}}}+\ip f{(g_{\mathrm{reg}})_{>T}}=\ip f{(g_{\mathrm{reg}})_{>T}}$, the low-pass being self-adjoint and the supports disjoint; their sum is $\ip f{h_{>T}}$ with $h=2Ps+g_{\mathrm{reg}}$. Inside the window the eigen-equation reads $2P\sinh(y/2)=K_\infty f_{\mathrm{int}}+K_Pf+e_{\mathrm{in}}-\lambda f$, where $f_{\mathrm{int}}$ is the interior part of $f$ and $e_{\mathrm{in}}$ the bounded remainder of $K_\infty$ on the edge profile; outside, $g_{\mathrm{reg}}=K_\infty f_{\mathrm{int}}+K_Pf+e_{\mathrm{out}}$ with $e_{\mathrm{out}}=(K_\infty\varphi)+CL^{1/2}$. The part $K_\infty f_{\mathrm{int}}+K_Pf$ is smooth across the edge, so $h$ is a smooth function plus the edge terms $e_{\mathrm{in}}-\lambda f$ and $e_{\mathrm{out}}$, which are $O(C)$, continuous at the edge by Lemma~\ref{lem:cont}, and of the form $C\times$(a function with an expansion in powers of $L^{-1/2}$). The high-pass of the smooth part against $f$ is $O(T^{-N})$; the high-pass of the $O(C)$ edge terms against $f$, whose own edge is $CL^{-1/2}$, is $O(C^2/(TL))$ by the computation of Lemma~\ref{lem:overlap} with $L^{-1/2}$ in place of $L^{1/2}$.
\end{proof}

So the sum rule is the tail identity with two facts about the edge: the overlap of the leak with the singular force is log-free by symmetry, and the regular force is continuous across the edge by the eigen-equation. The data say that the $O(1/L)$ of the corollary is small in practice (the zero sums sit on the law to one percent from five horizons on, where $L(1/T)\approx2$); its coefficient is not computed here. The corollary is, however, only the far-field form of something more robust.

\begin{computation}[the tail law is analytic]\label{comp:analytic}
\texttt{rh\_sum\_rule.py}. (i) On the FEM minimizers with primes, the edge law $CL(\delta)^{-1/2}$ with the $C,\beta$ of Computation~\ref{comp:hadamard} is not yet accurate at the scales that the heights of Computation~\ref{comp:tail} probe: $f(a-\delta)^2L(\delta)/C^2$ at $\delta=1/(k\Tst)$ is $1.48,\,2.04,\,3.20,\,3.17$ for $k=e$ at $a=0.4,\,0.45,\,0.5,\,a_3$, $1.20,\,1.33,\,1.54,\,1.43$ at $k=10$, and reaches $1.02$--$1.06$ only at $k=100$; yet the tail law holds at one percent from five horizons. (ii) On the prime-free critical points of Computation~\ref{comp:hadamard} ($a=0.3,\,0.4,\,0.5,\,0.6$; the form $W_\infty-2P^2$, whose floor is $+0.223,\,-0.078,\,-0.322,\,-0.536$), with $\lambda'$ the matrix-side derivative and no zero anywhere, $T\cdot\frac1{2\pi}\int_{|t|>T}|F|^2(\Psi_\infty-\lambda)\,dt$ over $-\lambda'/\pi$ is
\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}
\toprule
$a$ & $T/\Tst=3$ & $5$ & $10$ & $20$ & $50$ & $100$\\
\midrule
$0.3$ & $0.986$ & $0.980$ & $1.014$ & $1.008$ & $1.002$ & $0.999$\\
$0.4$ & $0.978$ & $1.020$ & $1.009$ & $1.001$ & $1.000$ & $1.000$\\
$0.5$ & $0.998$ & $1.000$ & $0.997$ & $0.997$ & $1.001$ & $1.001$\\
$0.6$ & $1.012$ & $1.007$ & $1.004$ & $1.001$ & $0.999$ & $1.000$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The law $T\,\frac1{2\pi}\int_{|t|>T}|F|^2(\Psi-\lambda)\to-\lambda'/\pi$ is therefore a property of critical points of window forms with the logarithmic symbol, holding to one percent from three horizons on whatever the sign of the floor, and the zeros enter only through (S$_\kappa$): they sample this analytic tail at their density. (iii) The sampling constant $M(\kappa)$ of (S$_\kappa$), the density integral over the zero sum above $\kappa\Tst$ on the FEM minimizers with primes, is $8.9,\,1.53,\,1.39,\,1.03,\,1.01,\,1.01,\,1.06,\,1.05$ at $a=0.5$, $3.6,\,0.91,\,1.14,\,1.09,\,1.08,\,1.07,\,1.07,\,1.03$ at $a=0.45$ and $1.8,\,0.73,\,1.16,\,1.01,\,1.05,\,1.05,\,1.08,\,1.03$ at $a=0.4$, for $\kappa=1,\,1.5,\,2,\,e,\,3,\,4,\,5,\,10$: above the exhaustion height the zeros sample the tail with constant one to within ten percent, and below two horizons they do not.
\end{computation}

Two things follow. The finite-height sum rule is analytic and has an exact form: the dilation derivative is itself an edge pairing, and the tail is its remainder at height $T$.

\begin{proposition}[the dilation derivative as an edge pairing]\label{prop:pairing}
Let $f$ be a critical point as in Proposition~\ref{prop:tailid}, $\mathcal D(f)$ the dilation form \eqref{eq:Dtilde}, and $(xf)'=f+xf'$ the dilation generator, an $L^1$ function supported in the window. For $T>0$ let $((xf)')_T$ be its low-pass at height $T$ and $B_T=\ip{g_{\mathrm{out}}}{((xf)')_T}$. Then for every $T$
\[
\mathcal D(f)\;=\;\mathrm{tail}(T)+\frac1\pi T|F(T)|^2\big(\Psi(T)-\lambda\big)+\mathrm{tail}_D(T)-4P\,\ip{s}{((xf)')_{>T}}+2B_T,
\]
with $\mathrm{tail}(T)=\frac1{2\pi}\int_{|t|>T}|F|^2(\Psi-\lambda)$ and $\mathrm{tail}_D(T)=\frac1{2\pi}\int_{|t|>T}|F|^2\,t\Psi'$. In particular $B_T$ converges, $\mathcal D(f)=2B_\infty$, and by Theorem~\ref{thm:identity} (Proposition~\ref{prop:dini} for the exact form) and the boundary law (Conjecture~\ref{conj:hadamard}, proved under the edge law with derivatives in Proposition~\ref{prop:flux})
\[
-a\lambda'=2\,\lim_{T\to\infty}\ip{g_{\mathrm{out}}}{((xf)')_T},\qquad B_\infty=aC^2 .
\]
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Write $\mathcal D=\frac1{2\pi}\int|F|^2t\Psi'+2P^2+2PM$ and cut the integral at $|t|=T$. On $|t|<T$ integrate by parts: $\frac1{2\pi}\int_{|t|<T}|F|^2t\Psi'=\frac1\pi T|F(T)|^2\Psi(T)-\frac1{2\pi}\int_{|t|<T}\Psi|F|^2-\frac1{2\pi}\int_{|t|<T}\Psi\,t(|F|^2)'$. The second term is $-(\lambda+2P^2)+\frac1{2\pi}\int_{|t|>T}\Psi|F|^2$. In the third, $\Psi\,t(|F|^2)'=2\Ree\big[\overline{\Psi F}\,tF'\big]$ and $\Psi F=\lambda F+2P\Sigma+G$ by the eigen-equation; since $tF'=-\widehat{(xf)'}$, Parseval gives $-\frac1{2\pi}\int_{|t|<T}\Psi t(|F|^2)'=-\lambda\big[\tfrac1\pi T|F(T)|^2-1+\tfrac1{2\pi}\int_{|t|>T}|F|^2\big]+4P\ip{s}{((xf)')_T}+2\ip{g_{\mathrm{out}}}{((xf)')_T}$. Collecting, and using $\ip s{(xf)'}=-M/2$ (integration by parts, $f(\pm a)=0$) to write $4P\ip s{((xf)')_T}=-2PM-4P\ip s{((xf)')_{>T}}$, the terms $\pm\lambda$, $\pm2P^2$ and $\pm2PM$ cancel and the identity follows. As $T\to\infty$ every term but $2B_T$ tends to $0$ ($\mathrm{tail}$ and $\mathrm{tail}_D$ since $|F|^2\Psi$ is integrable; the boundary term since $t|F|^2\Psi\to0$; the polar term since $s_{>T}\to0$ in $L^2$ and $(xf)'\in L^1\cap L^\infty$ away from the edge with the edge singularity $\delta^{-1}L^{-3/2}$ integrable), so $B_T\to\mathcal D/2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[the tail as the remainder of the pairing]\label{cor:remainder}
$\mathrm{tail}(T)=2(B_\infty-B_T)+4P\ip{s}{((xf)')_{>T}}-\frac1\pi T|F(T)|^2(\Psi(T)-\lambda)-\mathrm{tail}_D(T)$. The polar term is not small: the high-pass of $(xf)'$ carries the edge singularity $\delta^{-1}L^{-3/2}$, whose Gibbs layer against the jump of $s$ gives $-2aPC\sinh(a/2)L(1/T)^{-1/2}(1+o(1))$, decaying only logarithmically, and $2(B_\infty-B_T)$ cancels it at that order, as in Corollary~\ref{cor:sumrule}; the tail is the $O(1/T)$ remainder of that cancellation. The identity is the dilation identity in Fourier form and does not by itself make the law easier; its value is the exact expression of $-a\lambda'$ as an edge pairing.
\end{corollary}

\begin{computation}[the pairing identity on the prime-free critical point]\label{comp:pairing}
\texttt{rh\_cutoff\_identity.py}, on the prime-free FEM critical point at $a=0.5$, every term computed independently: $\mathcal D=D_\infty+2P^2+2PM=1.129084$ from the profile ($D_\infty=1.020022$, $P=0.134363$, $M=0.271485$), against $-a\lambda'=1.129075$ from the ball-arithmetic engine; $B_T=\ip{g_{\mathrm{out}}}{((xf)')_T}$ from the archimedean kernel in $x$-space and the inverse sine transform of $-tS'(t)$, $B_T=0.523,\,0.549,\,0.550,\,0.551$ at $T/\Tst=3,\,5,\,10,\,20$ against $\mathcal D/2=0.5645$; the right side of Proposition~\ref{prop:pairing} over $\mathcal D$ is $1.010,\,1.020,\,1.020,\,1.011$, the two percent being the resolution of the leak of $(xf)'$, whose edge singularity is $\delta^{-1}L^{-3/2}$, on the $y$-grid; and $\mathrm{tail}(T)$ is $0.997,\,0.998,\,0.992,\,0.989$ of $\mathcal D/(\pi aT)$. The polar term is $-0.047,\,-0.044,\,-0.039,\,-0.035$, five times the tail and decaying like $L(1/T)^{-1/2}$ as the corollary says, so $B_\infty-B_T$ is dominated by its cancellation; separating the $O(1/T)$ remainder needs $B_T$ to a part in ten thousand, beyond this computation.
\end{computation}

The sharpest form of the sum rule is local in $t$, and it is exact.

\begin{computation}[the envelope law]\label{comp:envelope}
The average of $t^2(\Psi(t)-\lambda)|F(t)|^2$ over a window of width $4\pi/a$ centred at $T$ (two periods of the interference of the two edges), divided by $-\lambda'$, on the FEM critical points (\texttt{rh\_sum\_rule.py}):
\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccccccc@{}}
\toprule
& $T/\Tst=1$ & $2$ & $3$ & $5$ & $10$ & $20$ & $50$ & $100$ & $300$\\
\midrule
$a=0.5$, no primes & $1.197$ & $1.016$ & $1.012$ & $1.001$ & $1.001$ & $1.000$ & $1.000$ & $1.000$ & $1.000$\\
$a=0.3$, no primes & $-2.81$ & $1.025$ & $1.112$ & $1.016$ & $1.006$ & $1.001$ & $1.000$ & $1.000$ & $1.001$\\
$a=0.5$, primes & $-6.30$ & $2.244$ & $1.270$ & $1.046$ & $1.006$ & $1.021$ & $1.005$ & $1.011$ & $1.008$\\
$a=0.4$, primes & $0.782$ & $1.876$ & $1.035$ & $1.145$ & $1.013$ & $0.957$ & $1.032$ & $0.993$ & $0.975$\\
\bottomrule

Without primes the local envelope equals $-\lambda'$ to a part in a thousand from ten horizons on and to $0.1\%$ from twenty, over two decades of height, with no logarithmic drift; with primes it fluctuates by a few percent about $1$, the cosines of the symbol beating against the interference of the edges.
\end{computation}

\begin{conjecture}[Wiener--Hopf form of the edge]\label{conj:wh}
Let $f$ be a critical point on $[-a,a]$ and $\sigma_\infty=\Psi_\infty-\lambda$. For a real even symbol $\sigma$ with finitely many real zeros $\pm t_j$ write $\tilde\sigma=\sigma\prod_j(t^2+t_j^2)/(t^2-t_j^2)>0$ and $\tilde\sigma=\sigma_+\sigma_-$ for its canonical factorization, $\sigma_\pm=\tilde\sigma^{1/2}e^{\pm i\theta}$ on the line with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:theta}
\theta(t)=\tfrac12\,\mathcal H[\log\tilde\sigma](t)=\frac t\pi\int_0^\infty\frac{\log\tilde\sigma(\tau)-\log\tilde\sigma(t)}{t^2-\tau^2}\,d\tau ,
\end{equation}
odd in $t$, so that $\sigma_+$ continues analytically without zeros to the upper half-plane and $\sigma_-$ to the lower. (i) Without primes ($\sigma=\sigma_\infty$) there are a height $T_0(a)$ and a real constant $\kappa$ such that, with $S(t)=\int_0^af\sin(tx)\,dx$ ($F=2iS$),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:whform}
S(t)=-\frac{|A|\cos\big(ta+\theta(t)-\kappa/t\big)}{t\,\tilde\sigma(t)^{1/2}}\big(1+O(t^{-2})\big),\qquad |A|^2=-\tfrac12\lambda',\qquad t>T_0 ;
\end{equation}
equivalently $F(t)=A(t)e^{ita}/(t\sigma_-(t))-\overline{A(t)}e^{-ita}/(t\sigma_+(t))+O(t^{-3})$ with $A(t)=-i|A|e^{-i\kappa/t}$: the amplitude of each edge is purely imaginary up to the phase $\kappa/t$, and there is no free phase. (ii) With the entries $n$ inside the window ($\log n<2a$; $c_n=\Lambda(n)n^{-1/2}$) each edge is accompanied by its echoes, at $a-\log n$, $a-\log n-\log m,\dots$, as long as they stay inside the window. For a single entry with $a<\log n<2a$ there is one echo; the symbol of the edge is $\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}=\sigma_\infty-c_n^2/\sigma_\infty$, and with $\rho(t)=c_n/\sigma_\infty(t)$, $\theta_{\mathrm{eff}}$ the phase \eqref{eq:theta} of $\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:echo}
S(t)=-\frac{|A|\,\big|1+\rho e^{-it\log n}\big|\,\cos\big(ta+\theta_{\mathrm{eff}}(t)+\arg(1+\rho e^{-it\log n})-\kappa/t\big)}{t\,\tilde\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}(t)^{1/2}}\big(1+O(t^{-2})\big).
\end{equation}
To first order in $\rho$, \eqref{eq:echo} is \eqref{eq:whform} with the symbol $\sigma_a=\Psi_a-\lambda$ of the window, $\Psi_a=\Psi_\infty-\sum_{\log n<2a}2\Lambda(n)n^{-1/2}\cos(t\log n)$. In both cases the local envelope of Computation~\ref{comp:envelope} is $2|A|^2=-\lambda'$, exactly in (i) and up to $O(\rho^2)$ in (ii), and the nulls of $S$ sit at $ta=\pi/2+k\pi-\vartheta(t)+\kappa/t$ with $\vartheta$ the phase of the respective form.
\end{conjecture}

The form comes from the two edges as two half-line problems, and the argument is the classical one for a Wiener--Hopf equation with a symbol of order zero; no cutoff is needed to see it. Write the eigen-equation on the whole line, $(K-\lambda)f=2Ps\,1_{[-a,a]}+g_{\mathrm{out}}$ with $g_{\mathrm{out}}=Kf$ outside the window as in Proposition~\ref{prop:tailid}, transform it, $\sigma F=2P\Sigma+G$, and multiply by $e^{-ita}$. The function $\tilde F=e^{-ita}F=\int_{-2a}^0f(a+u)e^{itu}\,du$ is analytic and bounded in the lower half-plane and $O(1/t)$ (a ``$-$'' function); $\tilde G_+=\int_{u>0}g_{\mathrm{out}}(a+u)e^{itu}\,du$ is analytic in the upper half-plane (a ``$+$'' function) and $O(t^{-1}\log^{1/2}t)$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:cont}; and $\tilde H=e^{-ita}\big(2P\Sigma+\int_{x<-a}g_{\mathrm{out}}e^{itx}\,dx\big)$ is a ``$-$'' function, $\tilde H=-2iP\sinh(a/2)/(t-i0)+e^{-2ita}\Phi_-(t)+O(t^{-2})$, the first term the step of the polar term at the edge and $\Phi_-=O(t^{-1}\log^{1/2}t)$ the far edge, its polar step and its outside force. The primes outside the window drop out: for $\log n>2a$ the shifted copies $f(x\mp\log n)$ lie entirely outside the window, so they are part of $g_{\mathrm{out}}$, and their transforms $e^{\pm it\log n}F$ reproduce the term $-2\Lambda(n)n^{-1/2}\cos(t\log n)F$ of $\sigma F$. So, exactly,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:whequation}
\sigma_a\tilde F=\tilde H+\tilde G_+ ,
\end{equation}
with the symbol $\sigma_a$ of the window, the partial copies of the entries inside the window (the part of $f(x-\log n)$ on $x>a$, of $f(x+\log n)$ on $x<-a$) staying in $\tilde G_+$ and $\tilde H$ on their sides.

\emph{Without primes.} Divide \eqref{eq:whequation} by $\sigma_+$ and split $\tilde H/\sigma_+$ additively into its parts analytic in the two half-planes, $[\cdot]_-+[\cdot]_+$. The function $E=\sigma_-\tilde F-[\tilde H/\sigma_+]_-=\tilde G_+/\sigma_++[\tilde H/\sigma_+]_+$ is entire and tends to zero in every direction, since $\sigma_\pm\sim(\log t)^{1/2}$, so $E\equiv0$ and $\tilde F=[\tilde H/\sigma_+]_-/\sigma_-$. For the polar step, $[1/((t-i0)\sigma_+(t))]_-=1/((t-i0)\sigma_+(0))$; for the far edge, $[e^{-2ita}\Phi_-/\sigma_+]_-=e^{-2ita}\Phi_-/\sigma_+-\sum_jc_j/(t-z_j)$ by closing the Cauchy integral in the lower half-plane, the sum running over the singularities $z_j$ of $1/\sigma_+=\sigma_-/\sigma$ there, the complex zeros of $\sigma$, with $c_j\propto e^{-2iz_ja}$. Hence
\[
\tilde F(t)=\frac{A(t)}{t\,\sigma_-(t)}+\frac{e^{-2ita}\Phi_-(t)}{\sigma(t)}+O(t^{-2}),\qquad A(t)=-\frac{2iP\sinh(a/2)}{\sigma_+(0)}-\sum_j\frac{c_j\,t}{t-z_j}=A_0+\frac{A_1}t+\dots ,
\]
the first term the right edge and the second the left one ($e^{-2ita}\Phi_-/\sigma=e^{-ita}F_L$). The symmetry $\tilde F(-t)=\overline{\tilde F(t)}$ of a real $f$, with $\sigma_-(-t)=\overline{\sigma_-(t)}$, gives $A(-t)=-\overline{A(t)}$: $A_0$ is purely imaginary and $A_1$ real, so $A(t)=A_0e^{-i\kappa/t}\big(1+O(t^{-2})\big)$ with $\kappa=A_1/|A_0|$, the first correction is a phase, and \eqref{eq:whform} follows. The far edge and the polar term fix $|A_0|$ (the sum over the $c_j$ is the coupling of the two edges, which is the quantization of $\lambda(a)$), and $|A_0|^2=-\lambda'/2$ is the boundary law $\lambda'=-2C^2$ read as a flux: the $x$-space asymptotics of $1/\sigma_-$ is the edge profile $L(\delta)^{-1/2}$, so $|A_0|=C$. All the logarithms of the edge profile are inside $\sigma_-$, which is why the profile of Computation~\ref{comp:analytic}(i) can be $1.5$--$3$ times above its asymptotic law at scale $1/T$ while the tail law holds at that $T$ to one percent; Proposition~\ref{prop:tail} and Corollary~\ref{cor:sumrule} are the far field of this structure. The real zeros $\pm t_j$ of $\sigma$ are the frequencies of the standing waves $e^{\pm it_jx}$ that solve $(K-\lambda)u=0$ on the line and make up the interior of the critical point; with them the homogeneous problem has solutions, $E$ is a rational function with simple poles at $\pm t_j$ instead of zero, and the standing-wave amplitudes enter $A_0$ and $\kappa$ (the $\arctan(t_j/t)$ of the zeros' factors are part of $\kappa$). Proposition~\ref{prop:whexact} and Corollary~\ref{cor:edgeform} below make this rigorous, for every $L^2$ critical point of the prime-free form and without any hypothesis on the edge: the decay of the three functions needs only $f\in L^2$, and the factor of a symbol growing like $\log t$ is the outer function of $\tilde\sigma^{1/2}$.

\emph{With primes.} An entry inside the window shifts the window onto itself: $(K_\infty-\lambda)f=2Ps+\sum_nc_n\big(f(x-\log n)+f(x+\log n)\big)$ on the window, $K_\infty$ the archimedean part of $K$. The right edge's singularity is thereby copied to $a-\log n$, the copy to $a-\log n-\log m$, and so on while the points stay inside the window: a finite set of echoes, a chain for each entry, and the mirror chains from the left edge. Take one entry with $a<\log n<2a$, so that each chain has one link, and split $f$ near $a$ and near $a-\log n$ into an edge piece $f_R$ and an echo piece $f_e$ with a partition of unity. At the echo point $(K_\infty-\lambda)f_e=c_nf_R(x+\log n)+{}$smooth, so $F_e=(c_n/\sigma_\infty)e^{-it\log n}F_R$ up to rapidly decaying terms. At the edge $(K_\infty-\lambda)f_R=2Ps+c_nf_e(x-\log n)+{}$smooth on $x<a$, and the echo shifted back is a two-sided singularity at $a$ with transform $c_ne^{it\log n}F_e=(c_n^2/\sigma_\infty)F_R$; moved to the left it makes the edge a half-line problem with the real even symbol $\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}=\sigma_\infty-c_n^2/\sigma_\infty$, to which everything above applies: $\tilde F_R=A(t)/(t\sigma_{\mathrm{eff},-})$. Since $f$ is odd, $F=2i\,\mathrm{Im}(F_R+F_e)$, which is \eqref{eq:echo}. With the symbol $\sigma_a=\sigma_\infty-2c_n\cos(t\log n)$ of the window instead one has $\sigma_a^{-1/2}=\sigma_\infty^{-1/2}\big(1+\rho\cos(t\log n)\big)+O(\rho^2)$ and $\theta_a=\theta_\infty-\rho\sin(t\log n)+O(\rho^2)$, the same as \eqref{eq:echo} to first order in $\rho$: the Hilbert transform of the cosine is the echo. The difference is at second order and it is the window's: for a constant $\sigma_\infty$ the factorization $\sigma_\infty-2c_n\cos\tau=(\alpha-\beta e^{-i\tau})(\alpha-\beta e^{i\tau})$, $\alpha^2=\tfrac12\big(\sigma_\infty+\sqrt{\sigma_\infty^2-4c_n^2}\,\big)$, $\beta=c_n/\alpha$, expands $1/\sigma_{a,-}$ into the infinite chain $\alpha^{-1}\sum_k(c_n/\alpha^2)^ke^{-ik\tau}$, with $\alpha^2$ the fixed point of $\sigma\mapsto\sigma_\infty-c_n^2/\sigma$: the edge of a half-line with all its echoes, while the window cuts the chain where it leaves $[-a,a]$, and $\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is the continued fraction $\sigma_\infty-c_n^2/(\sigma_\infty-c_n^2/(\cdots))$ with as many levels as there are echoes inside. Part (iii) of the following computation measures exactly this.

\begin{computation}[the Wiener--Hopf phase]\label{comp:whphase}
On the prime-free critical point at $a=0.5$ of Computation~\ref{comp:analytic} (edge FEM, $486$ nodes to $10^{-12}a$, $\lambda=-0.32224$, $\lambda'=-2.25815$) the symbol $\sigma=\Psi_\infty-\lambda$ has the single real zero $t_\lambda=4.562$, and $\theta$ of \eqref{eq:theta} is computed by the trapezoidal rule on a grid of step $0.01$ to $8000$ and geometric to $10^{11}$ (self-test on $\log\frac{t^2+1}{t^2+4}$, whose phase is $\arctan\frac1t-\arctan\frac2t$: error $5\times10^{-10}$); \texttt{rh\_wiener\_hopf.py}, logs in \texttt{data/}. (i) \emph{The nulls.} $S$ has $319$ nulls $t_k$ between $2.8$ and $120$ horizons, $t_ka=\pi/2+k\pi+r_k$. The shift $r_k$ is $0.339$, $0.294$, $0.239$, $0.199$, $0.162$, $0.141$, $0.136$ at $3.2$, $5$, $10$, $20$, $50$, $100$, $120$ horizons, and $-\theta(t_k)$ is $0.259$, $0.243$, $0.214$, $0.187$, $0.157$, $0.138$, $0.134$; the difference $r_k+\theta(t_k)=0.080$, $0.051$, $0.025$, $0.013$, $0.005$, $0.0023$, $0.0022$ is $\kappa/t_k$ with $\kappa=4.34$ (fitted on $t\ge10\,\Tst$; against $t_\lambda=4.56$, the $\arctan(t_\lambda/t)$ of the zero's factor), and the residual $r_k+\theta(t_k)-\kappa/t_k$ has root mean square $1.2\times10^{-4}$ over all $319$ nulls, with no trend. The alternatives fail: a $1/t$ shift alone leaves $0.19$; the adiabatic value of the phase, $-\frac\pi4\,t\,\frac{d}{dt}\log\tilde\sigma\approx-\frac{\pi}{4\log(t/2\pi)}$, fitted as $c/\log(t/2\pi)$ (best $c=0.79$ against $\pi/4=0.785$), leaves $3.5\times10^{-3}$, thirty times the exact phase, its error being $0.029$, $0.018$, $0.010$ at $3.2$, $3.9$, $5$ horizons, where the Hilbert integral \eqref{eq:theta} differs from its local value by that much. A second mesh ($471$ nodes, coarser between $\delta=10^{-6}$ and $10^{-3}$) reproduces $r_k$ to $2\times10^{-3}$ up to $20$ horizons and drifts beyond, where its mesh at scale $1/t$ shows. (ii) \emph{Pointwise.} With $|A|=(-\lambda'/2)^{1/2}=1.0626$ and the fitted $\kappa$, nothing else adjusted, the relative $L^2$ distance over one interference period $4\pi/a$ between $S$ and $-|A|\cos(ta+\theta-\kappa/t)/(t\tilde\sigma^{1/2})$ is $4.5\times10^{-3}$, $1.5\times10^{-3}$, $3.4\times10^{-4}$, $8\times10^{-5}$, $3\times10^{-5}$ at $3$, $5$, $10$, $20$, $50$ horizons, decaying like $T^{-2}$ ($2.7\times10^{-4}$ at $100$, the mesh); with $\kappa=0$ it is $0.093$, $0.052$, $0.026$, $0.013$, $0.005$, the phase $\kappa/t$ itself, and with $\theta=0$ it is $0.36$, $0.29$, $0.24$, $0.20$, $0.16$. (iii) \emph{With the prime $2$} (the critical point of $W-2P^2$ at $a=0.5$, $\lambda=1.9\times10^{-4}$, $\lambda'=-0.011236$; the entry $2$ is the only one inside the window, with $a<\log2<2a$, so each edge has one echo, at $\pm(a-\log2)=\pm0.193$). The window's symbol $\Psi_\infty-0.980\cos(t\log2)-\lambda$ has three real zeros, $3.31$, $6.95$, $10.53$, and its phase oscillates at the prime's frequency with amplitude about $\rho=0.490/\sigma_\infty$: $-\theta_a(t_k)=0.16$, $0.29$, $0.05$, $0.13$, $0.13$, $0.21$, $0.23$, $0.07$ at $9.8$, $15$, $20$, $30$, $50$, $70$, $100$, $120$ horizons. The nulls follow it: $r_k+\theta_a(t_k)-\kappa/t_k$ has root mean square $0.010$ over $3$--$120$ horizons ($\kappa=62.4$), against $0.25$--$0.32$ for $r_k$, $0.11$--$0.20$ for $r_k+\theta_a(t_k)$, $0.08$--$0.13$ for the smooth law $c/\log(t/2\pi)$, and $0.070$ for the phase of the prime-free symbol at the same $\lambda$ plus $\kappa/t$; so the wobble of the nulls is the Hilbert transform of the prime's cosine, and the form \eqref{eq:whform} with $\sigma_a$ is within $0.14$, $0.051$, $0.024$, $0.016$, $0.010$ of $S$ at $3$, $5$, $10$, $20$, $50$ horizons, a percent-level remainder that does not decay. The echo form \eqref{eq:echo} removes it: with $\sigma_{\mathrm{eff}}=\sigma_\infty-0.240/\sigma_\infty$ (real zeros $3.86$, $6.29$, $10.26$) and $\kappa=62.2$, the null residual is $1.1\times10^{-3}$ in root mean square over the $204$ nulls between $5$ and $80$ horizons (largest $3.6\times10^{-3}$; the window's symbol leaves $9.8\times10^{-3}$ there, no shift $0.32$), rising to $0.02$ above $80$ horizons where the mesh at scale $1/t$ shows, and the pointwise distance is $0.010$, $0.0021$, $0.0007$ at $10$, $20$, $50$ horizons, decaying like $T^{-2}$ ($0.0099$ at $100$, the mesh; $0.15$ and $0.044$ at $3$ and $5$, where the interior of this critical point, with $\lambda\approx0$ and three standing waves, is still felt). So the second-order remainder of the window's symbol is the echo that the window cuts off, and the few-percent modulation of the envelope in Computation~\ref{comp:envelope} is its trace.
\end{computation}

The derivation is a theorem for the prime-free form, for every $L^2$ critical point and with no hypothesis on the edge. We need two facts about the symbol.

\begin{lemma}[the factor of a logarithmic symbol]\label{lem:factor}
Let $\tilde\sigma$ be even, positive and $C^2$ on $\R$ with $c_1\le\tilde\sigma(t)\le c_2\log(e+|t|)$, $(\log\tilde\sigma)'\in L^2$ and $(\log\tilde\sigma)''$ bounded, and let $\theta$ be given by \eqref{eq:theta}. Then $\sigma_+=\tilde\sigma^{1/2}e^{i\theta}$ is the boundary value of the outer function of $\tilde\sigma^{1/2}$: it extends to an analytic function without zeros in the upper half-plane, continuous up to the line, with $c\le|\sigma_+(z)|\le C(\log(e+|z|))^{1/2}$ there; $\sigma_-(z)=\overline{\sigma_+(\bar z)}$ is analytic without zeros in the lower half-plane with the same bounds; $\sigma_+\sigma_-=\tilde\sigma$ on the line; $\theta$ is odd and $C^1$ with $\theta,\theta'$ bounded, so that $1/\sigma_\pm$ are bounded with bounded derivative on the line.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
$w=\tilde\sigma^{1/2}$ has $\log w\in L^1(d\tau/(1+\tau^2))$, so the outer function $O(z)=\exp\big[\frac1{\pi i}\int\big(\frac1{\tau-z}-\frac\tau{1+\tau^2}\big)\log w(\tau)\,d\tau\big]$ is analytic and zero-free in the upper half-plane, $|O(t)|=w(t)$ on the line and $\log|O(x+iy)|=P_y*\log w\,(x)$ is the Poisson integral \cite[Ch.~II]{garnett}. Its argument on the line is the conjugate function of $\log w$; for even $w$ the term $\tau/(1+\tau^2)$ drops out by parity and the two half-lines combine into \eqref{eq:theta}, which is odd and vanishes at $0$; so $O=\sigma_+$, the unimodular constant of the outer function being fixed by $\theta(0)=0$. Since $\log w\ge\frac12\log c_1$, $|O(z)|\ge c$. Since $\log w(\tau)\le\frac12\log\log(e+|\tau|)+C$ and $\log\log(e+|\tau|)\le\log\log(e+|z|)+\log\big(1+|\tau-x|/(e+|z|)\big)$ for $z=x+iy$, the Poisson integral gives $\log|O(z)|\le\frac12\log\log(e+|z|)+C+\frac12\int P_y(s)\log(1+|s|/y)\,ds$, and the last integral equals $\int P_1(s)\log(1+|s|)\,ds<\infty$. Finally $\theta'=\frac12\mathcal H[(\log\tilde\sigma)']$, and the Hilbert transform of a $C^1$ function in $L^2$ with bounded derivative is bounded (split the integral at $|\tau-t|=1$). Continuity up to the line follows from that of $\log w$ and $\theta$, and the statements for $\sigma_-$ are the reflection $z\mapsto\bar z$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[the kernel of $1/\sigma_+$ away from the origin]\label{lem:kernel}
Let $\sigma=\Psi_\infty-\lambda$ with $\lambda>\Psi_\infty(0)$, so that $\sigma$ has exactly one pair of simple real zeros $\pm t_\lambda$ ($\Psi_\infty$ is even and increasing on $t>0$, as $\frac d{dt}\Ree\psi(\frac14+\frac{it}2)=-\frac12\Imm\psi'(\frac14+\frac{it}2)>0$), and let $\tilde\sigma=\sigma\,(t^2+t_\lambda^2)/(t^2-t_\lambda^2)$. Then $\tilde\sigma$ satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:factor}; there is $\eta\in(0,\frac12)$ such that $\tilde\sigma$ is analytic and zero-free in the strip $-\eta\le\Imm z\le0$, so that $1/\sigma_+=\sigma_-/\tilde\sigma$ is bounded and analytic there; and the kernel of $1/\sigma_+$,
\[
k_+(\xi)=\frac1{2\pi}\int_\R\frac{e^{-it\xi}}{\sigma_+(t)}\,dt\qquad(\xi>0,\ \text{as an improper integral}),
\]
is a real $C^2$ function on $(0,\infty)$ with $|k_+^{(j)}(\xi)|\le C_\delta e^{-\eta\xi}$ on $\xi\ge\delta$, $j\le2$, for every $\delta>0$; $1/\sigma_+$ is the transform of the distribution $k_+$ supported on $[0,\infty)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
$\sigma$ is real-analytic and even with $\sigma(t)=\log(t/2\pi)-\lambda+O(t^{-2})$ and derivatives $O(t^{-j})$ by Stirling's formula, and $\sigma<0$ on $|t|<t_\lambda$, so $\tilde\sigma>0$, $\tilde\sigma\asymp\log(e+|t|)$ and $(\log\tilde\sigma)^{(j)}=O(t^{-j}/\log t)$: Lemma~\ref{lem:factor} applies. For $z=x-iy$, $0\le y\le\eta<\frac12$, both $\frac14+\frac{iz}2$ and $\frac14-\frac{iz}2$ have real part at least $\frac14-\frac\eta2>0$, so Stirling's formula holds uniformly and $\sigma(z)=\log(|x|/2\pi)-\lambda+O(1/|x|)$ there; hence $|\tilde\sigma(z)|\ge\frac12\log|x|$ for $|x|\ge X_0$, and on the compact rectangle $|x|\le X_0$ the continuous function $\tilde\sigma$, which has no zero on the real segment, has none for $\eta$ small. So $1/\sigma_+=\sigma_-/\tilde\sigma$ is analytic in the strip and bounded by Lemma~\ref{lem:factor}. Its derivatives on the line $\Imm z=-\eta$ decay: $\log\sigma_-$ is the Cauchy integral of $\log\tilde\sigma^{1/2}$, whose $j$-th derivative at $z$ is $O(\log(e+|z|)/|z|^j)$ (split the integral at $|\tau-x|=|z|/2$ and use $\frac12\log\log$ growth outside and the bound $|(\log\tilde\sigma)'|\le C/(|x|\log|x|)$ inside), and $\tilde\sigma^{(j)}=O(|x|^{-j})$. The improper integral defining $k_+$ converges after two integrations by parts, since $(1/\sigma_+)''=O(t^{-2})$ on the line; shifting the contour to $\Imm t=-\eta$ (the vertical connectors vanish as $|\Ree t|\to\infty$ because $1/\sigma_+$ is bounded and $e^{-it\xi}$ is) gives $k_+(\xi)=\frac{e^{-\eta\xi}}{2\pi}\int e^{-ix\xi}\sigma_+(x-i\eta)^{-1}dx$, and $j+2$ integrations by parts on that line, with the derivative bounds just stated, give $|k_+^{(j)}(\xi)|\le C_\delta e^{-\eta\xi}$ for $\xi\ge\delta$. That $k_+$ is real follows from $\sigma_+(-t)=\overline{\sigma_+(t)}$, and that it is supported on $[0,\infty)$ from the analyticity and boundedness of $1/\sigma_+$ in the upper half-plane (Paley--Wiener).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[exact Wiener--Hopf representation of a prime-free critical point]\label{prop:whexact}
Let $f\in L^2(-a,a)$ be odd and a critical point of $W_\infty-2P^2$: $K_\infty f=\lambda f+2Ps$ on $(-a,a)$, $P=\ip fs$, with $\lambda>\Psi_\infty(0)$, and let $\sigma$, $t_\lambda$, $\tilde\sigma$, $\sigma_\pm$ be as in Lemma~\ref{lem:kernel}. With $g_{\mathrm{out}}=K_\infty f$ outside the window define on the line
\[
\tilde F=e^{-ita}F,\qquad \tilde G_+=\int_0^\infty g_{\mathrm{out}}(a+u)e^{itu}\,du,\qquad \tilde H=e^{-ita}\Big(2P\Sigma+\int_{y<-a}g_{\mathrm{out}}(y)e^{ity}\,dy\Big),
\]
and $\hat H_-=\tilde H+t_\lambda\big[\frac{\tilde H(t)-\tilde H(t_\lambda)}{t-t_\lambda}-\frac{\tilde H(t)-\tilde H(-t_\lambda)}{t+t_\lambda}\big]$. Then $\hat H_-/\sigma_+\in H^1(\R)$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:whexact}
\tilde F=\frac{P_-\big[\hat H_-/\sigma_+\big]}{\sigma_-}\qquad\text{on }\R ,
\end{equation}
where $P_\pm=\frac12(I\pm i\mathcal H)$ are the Riesz projections onto the boundary values of $H^2$ of the upper and lower half-planes.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
\emph{The equation.} As in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:tailid}, $K_\infty f=\lambda f+2Ps+g_{\mathrm{out}}$ on the line, so $\sigma F=2P\Sigma+G$ with $G=\widehat{g_{\mathrm{out}}}$; multiplying by $e^{-ita}$ and splitting $G$ at the window, $\sigma\tilde F=\tilde H+\tilde G_+$. Since $J_\Gamma(s)=\frac1{2|s|}+O(1)$ near $0$ and $J_\Gamma(s)=O(e^{-|s|/2})$, $g_{\mathrm{out}}(a+u)$ is $-\frac12$ times the Hilbert transform of $f$ at $a+u$ plus a bounded, exponentially decaying function, so $g_{\mathrm{out}}\in L^1\cap L^2$ outside the window, with $yg_{\mathrm{out}}\in L^2$. Hence: $\tilde F(z)=\int_{-2a}^0f(a+u)e^{izu}\,du$ is entire and bounded by $\|f\|_1$ in the closed lower half-plane; $\tilde G_+$ is analytic in the upper half-plane, continuous on its closure, bounded by $\|g_{\mathrm{out}}\|_1$, and tends to zero at infinity there (Riemann--Lebesgue for the family $g_{\mathrm{out}}(a+\cdot)e^{-yu}$, $y\ge0$, which is compact in $L^1$); $\tilde H$ is analytic in the lower half-plane, continuous on its closure, bounded, and its restriction to the line is in $H^1(\R)$, being the transform of a function $h$ with $h,\,yh\in L^2$.

\emph{The real zeros.} $\sigma(\pm t_\lambda)=0$ forces $(\tilde H+\tilde G_+)(\pm t_\lambda)=0$. Multiply the equation by $(t^2+t_\lambda^2)/(t^2-t_\lambda^2)=1+t_\lambda\big[\frac1{t-t_\lambda}-\frac1{t+t_\lambda}\big]$ and write each product $t_\lambda\tilde H(t)/(t\mp t_\lambda)$ as a difference quotient plus $t_\lambda\tilde H(\pm t_\lambda)/(t\mp t_\lambda)$, likewise for $\tilde G_+$: the terms with real poles cancel, and $\tilde\sigma\tilde F=\hat H_-+\hat G_+$ with $\hat G_+$ built from $\tilde G_+$ as $\hat H_-$ from $\tilde H$. The difference quotients are analytic where $\tilde H$ (resp.\ $\tilde G_+$) is, continuous up to the line, bounded, and $O(1/t)$ with derivative $O(t^{-2})$; so $\hat H_-$ and $\hat G_+$ have the properties listed for $\tilde H$ and $\tilde G_+$.

\emph{Liouville.} Put $\varphi=\hat H_-/\sigma_+$; it is in $H^1(\R)$ because $1/\sigma_+$ is bounded with bounded derivative (Lemma~\ref{lem:factor}). Then $P_\pm\varphi\in H^2$ of the respective half-planes have boundary values in $H^1(\R)\subset C(\R)\cap L^\infty$, are the Poisson integrals of these boundary values, hence bounded and continuous on the closed half-planes, and $|P_\pm\varphi(x\pm iy)|\le\|\varphi\|_2(4\pi y)^{-1/2}$. Define $E=\sigma_-\tilde F-P_-\varphi$ on the closed lower half-plane and $E=\hat G_+/\sigma_++P_+\varphi$ on the closed upper one. On the line the two agree, since their difference is $\big(\tilde\sigma\tilde F-\hat H_--\hat G_+\big)/\sigma_+=0$. So $E$ is continuous on $\C$ and analytic off the line, hence entire; it is bounded by $C(\log(e+|z|))^{1/2}$ (below by the bounds on $\sigma_-$, $\tilde F$ and $P_-\varphi$; above by those on $\hat G_+$, $1/\sigma_+$ and $P_+\varphi$), so it is constant by Cauchy's estimate, and $E(iy)\to0$ as $y\to\infty$. Hence $E\equiv0$, which is \eqref{eq:whexact}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[the form of the edge]\label{cor:edgeform}
In the setting of Proposition~\ref{prop:whexact}, fix $b>0$ and collect the far edge in
\[
\Phi_-(t)=\int_0^\infty g_{\mathrm{out}}(-a-v)e^{-itv}\,dv-\frac{2iP\sinh(a/2)}{t-ib},
\]
so that $e^{-2ita}\Phi_-$ is the transform of the part $\mathrm{far}(u)=\big(g_{\mathrm{out}}(a+u)+2P\sinh(a/2)e^{b(u+2a)}\big)\mathbf 1_{u<-2a}$ of the source of $\tilde H$. Then, exactly on the line,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:edgeform}
\tilde F(t)=\frac{A(t)}{t\,\sigma_-(t)}+\frac{e^{-2ita}\,\Phi_-(t)}{\tilde\sigma(t)},\qquad A(t)=t\,P_-\Big[\frac{\hat H_--e^{-2ita}\Phi_-}{\sigma_+}\Big](t)-t\,P_+\Big[\frac{e^{-2ita}\Phi_-}{\sigma_+}\Big](t),
\end{equation}
with $A(t)=A_0+O(\log t/t)$, $A_0$ purely imaginary, and $A(-t)=-\overline{A(t)}$. Moreover
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mirror}
F(t)=\frac{A(t)e^{ita}}{t\,\sigma_-(t)}-\frac{\overline{A(t)}\,e^{-ita}}{t\,\sigma_+(t)}+O\big(t^{-2}(\log t)^{-1/2}\big),\qquad S(t)=\Imm\frac{A(t)e^{ita}}{t\,\sigma_-(t)}+O\big(t^{-2}(\log t)^{-1/2}\big),
\end{equation}
so that, writing $A(t)=-i|A_0|e^{-i\kappa(t)/t}(1+\varepsilon(t))$ with $\kappa$ real, $S(t)=-|A_0|\cos\big(ta+\theta(t)-\kappa(t)/t\big)\big/\big(t\tilde\sigma(t)^{1/2}\big)+O(t^{-2}(\log t)^{-1/2})+O(\varepsilon/t)$, $\kappa(t)=O(\log t)$ and $\varepsilon(t)=O(1/t)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
\eqref{eq:edgeform} is \eqref{eq:whexact} with $P_-[e^{-2ita}\Phi_-/\sigma_+]=e^{-2ita}\Phi_-/\sigma_+-P_+[e^{-2ita}\Phi_-/\sigma_+]$ and $\sigma_+\sigma_-=\tilde\sigma$; the function $e^{-2ita}\Phi_-/\sigma_+$ is in $L^2$ since $\mathrm{far}\in L^1\cap L^2$. The symmetry $A(-t)=-\overline{A(t)}$ follows from $\tilde F(-t)=\overline{\tilde F(t)}$, $\Phi_-(-t)=\overline{\Phi_-(t)}$, $\sigma_-(-t)=\overline{\sigma_-(t)}$ and the parity of $\tilde\sigma$.

\emph{The near part.} Integrating $2P\Sigma$ by parts, $\hat H_--e^{-2ita}\Phi_-=A'/(t-ib)+\Psi_2$ with $A'=-2iP\sinh(a/2)-t_\lambda\big(\tilde H(t_\lambda)-\tilde H(-t_\lambda)\big)$, purely imaginary because $\tilde H(-t)=\overline{\tilde H(t)}$, and $\Psi_2$ analytic in the lower half-plane, $O(t^{-2})$ on the line. Now $P_-\big[1/((t-ib)\sigma_+)\big]=1/((t-ib)\sigma_+(ib))$ exactly, because $\big[1/\sigma_+(t)-1/\sigma_+(ib)\big]/(t-ib)$ is in $H^2$ of the upper half-plane; and $\sigma_+(ib)>0$ (the outer function of an even weight is real and positive on the imaginary axis). Split the source of $\Psi_2$ with a smooth partition of unity into a part supported in $(-a,0]$ and a part supported in $u<-a/2$. The first is smooth on $(-a,0]$ and continuous at $0$ with a kink (the step of the polar term at the edge has been removed), so its transform $\psi$ satisfies $\psi=O(t^{-2})$, $\psi'=O(t^{-3})$, and for such $\psi$ one has $t\,P_-[\psi/\sigma_+](t)=-\frac i{2\pi}\int\psi/\sigma_++O(\log t/t)$: indeed $P_-=\frac12(I-i\mathcal H)$, $t\psi=O(1/t)$, and $t\mathcal H[\psi/\sigma_+](t)=\frac1\pi\int\psi/\sigma_++\frac1\pi\int\frac{\psi(\tau)\tau}{\sigma_+(\tau)(t-\tau)}\,d\tau$, where the last integral is $O(\log t/t)$ on $|\tau|<t/2$, $O(1/t)$ on $|\tau-t|<t/2$ (the principal value of $[m(\tau)-m(t)]/(t-\tau)$ with $m=\tau\psi/\sigma_+$, $m'=O(\tau^{-2})$) and $O(1/t)$ on $|\tau|>3t/2$. The integral $\int\psi/\sigma_+$ is real, as $\psi(-\tau)=\overline{\psi(\tau)}$ and $\sigma_+(-\tau)=\overline{\sigma_+(\tau)}$.

\emph{The far parts.} Every remaining piece of $\hat H_-$ (the far source of $\Phi_-$ and the second part of the source of $\Psi_2$) is the transform $\tilde\Phi$ of a real function $\mathrm{src}\in L^1\cap L^2$ supported in $u\le-\delta$ for some $\delta>0$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:kernel}, $\tilde\Phi/\sigma_+$ is the transform of $\mathrm{src}*k_+$, and $P_+[\tilde\Phi/\sigma_+]$ is the transform of its restriction to $u>0$, $r(u)=\int_{u'\le-\delta}\mathrm{src}(u')k_+(u-u')\,du'$, which uses $k_+$ only on $[\delta,\infty)$: $r$ is real, $C^2$ on $[0,\infty)$, and $r,r',r''\in L^1$. Integrating by parts, $\int_0^\infty r(u)e^{itu}\,du=ir(0^+)/t-r'(0^+)/t^2+o(t^{-2})$, so $-t\,P_+[\tilde\Phi/\sigma_+](t)=-ir(0^+)+O(1/t)$ with $r(0^+)$ real; and $t\,P_-[\tilde\Phi/\sigma_+]=t\tilde\Phi/\sigma_+-t\,P_+[\tilde\Phi/\sigma_+]$, where $t\tilde\Phi/\sigma_+=O(1/t)$ for the piece of $\Psi_2$ ($\tilde\Phi=O(t^{-2})$ there). Collecting, $A(t)=A_0+O(\log t/t)$ with $A_0=A'/\sigma_+(ib)-\frac i{2\pi}\int\psi/\sigma_+-i\sum r(0^+)$ purely imaginary.

\emph{The mirror.} From $\sigma\tilde F=\tilde H+\tilde G_+$ and \eqref{eq:edgeform}, $\tilde G_+=\sigma\tilde F-\tilde H=\frac{\sigma}{\tilde\sigma}\frac{\sigma_+A}t+\frac\sigma{\tilde\sigma}e^{-2ita}\Phi_--2P\tilde\Sigma-e^{-2ita}\big(\Phi_-+\tfrac{2iP\sinh(a/2)}{t-ib}\big)$, where $\tilde\Sigma=e^{-ita}\Sigma=-i\sinh(a/2)(1+e^{-2ita})/t+O(t^{-2})$ and $\sigma/\tilde\sigma=1-2t_\lambda^2/(t^2+t_\lambda^2)$; the terms in $e^{-2ita}$ cancel up to $O(t^{-2})$ ($\Phi_-$ is bounded), and $\tilde G_+(t)=\sigma_+(t)A(t)/t+2iP\sinh(a/2)/t+O(t^{-2}(\log t)^{1/2})$. Since $f$ is odd, $g_{\mathrm{out}}(-a-v)=-g_{\mathrm{out}}(a+v)$, so $\Phi_-=-\overline{\tilde G_+}-2iP\sinh(a/2)/(t-ib)=-\sigma_-\overline A/t+O(t^{-2}(\log t)^{1/2})$, and the second term of \eqref{eq:edgeform} is $-e^{-2ita}\overline{A(t)}/(t\sigma_+(t))+O(t^{-2}(\log t)^{-1/2})$. Multiplying by $e^{ita}$ gives \eqref{eq:mirror}; $F=2iS$ is purely imaginary, and the two terms are conjugate up to sign, so $S$ is the imaginary part of the first. The last statement is $A(-t)=-\overline{A(t)}$ read on $A(t)-A_0=\beta(t)/t$: $\beta(-t)=\overline{\beta(t)}$, so the even part of $\beta$ is real, a phase to first order, while its odd part, which is $O(1/t)$ by the expansions above, is imaginary.
\end{proof}

Corollary~\ref{cor:edgeform} is Conjecture~\ref{conj:wh}(i) except for three things it does not decide: the value $|A_0|^2=-\lambda'/2$, which is the boundary law (Propositions~\ref{prop:flux} and~\ref{prop:profile} prove it under (H$_\infty$), Corollary~\ref{cor:primefree}); the constancy of the phase $\kappa(t)$, for which the bound is $O(\log t)$ while the data of Computation~\ref{comp:whphase} give a constant to $10^{-4}$; and the rate, $O(\log t/t)$ against the measured $t^{-2}$. With an entry inside the window the same holds with the effective symbol of Proposition~\ref{prop:whfull}, and part (ii) of the conjecture, the echo form, is Corollary~\ref{cor:full}(iii); at every support with a finite echo set the effective symbol is the Schur complement of the echo tree, Proposition~\ref{prop:tree}, and the echo form is Corollary~\ref{cor:tree}(iii). Its content for the tail law is this: $\int_{|t|>T}|F|^2\tilde\sigma\,dt=8|A_0|^2\int_T^\infty\cos^2(ta+\theta-\kappa/t)\,dt/t^2\,(1+o(1))=4|A_0|^2/T\,(1+o(1))$, so for every prime-free critical point the tail energy of Corollary~\ref{cor:sumrule} equals $2|A_0|^2/(\pi T)$ at every height beyond the onset, and the tail law $-\lambda'/(\pi T)$ of Computation~\ref{comp:analytic} is the single identity $2|A_0|^2=-\lambda'$: with $|A_0|=C$, the boundary law. Item 2 of the program is therefore reduced to the flux identity, and to the echo form with primes.

\begin{heuristic}[why the effective height is tied to the horizon]\label{heur:why}
Below the horizon the zeros are sparser than the $a/\pi$ degrees of freedom per unit height of a function of type $a$, and the minimizer vanishes at all of them: their share of the floor is $10^{-5}$, $10^{-7}$, $10^{-13}$ at $a=0.6,0.8,1.0$. Above it, the excess of the zero density over the Nyquist density is $\log(t/\Tst)/2\pi$, the same function of $t/\Tst$ for every $a$; so, after scaling heights by the horizon, the minimizer faces the same problem at every support, which is why $T_{\mathrm{eff}}/\Tst$ should tend to a constant, and why the profiles $\vartheta(T/\Tst)$ should converge as $a$ grows. The only way for a function of type $a$ to be small at more points than it has freedom for is to steepen at the edge (the edge law and the Hadamard formula), and the edge amplitude feeds the tail at every height at once, at the rate $C^2/(\pi T)$; the minimizer balances the gain from hiding at one more zero just above the horizon against that cost, and the balance falls at a fixed multiple of $\Tst$. The spare freedom below the horizon is worth $\Tst/2\pi$ zeros, and the accumulated excess above it, $\int_{\Tst}^T\log(t/\Tst)\,dt/2\pi$, exhausts it at $T=e\Tst$; the measured median heights are below that and rising with $a$. Two things enter that no counting supplies: positivity of every term, which is RH, and the absence of gaps between zeros wider than the resolution $\pi/a$ above a few horizons, in which a shape could park its tail. The explanation is therefore conditional on where the zeros are; it says what Conjecture~\ref{conj:A} asserts about them, not why the prime side must produce it.
\end{heuristic}

The count in the heuristic has an exact form.

\begin{lemma}[exhaustion height]\label{lem:exhaust}
The number of zeros in $[-T,T]$ equals the Shannon number $2aT/\pi$ of the window $[-a,a]$ at bandwidth $T$, up to $O(\log T)$, exactly at $T=e\,\Tst(a)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
$2N(T)=\frac T\pi\big(\log\frac T{2\pi}-1\big)+O(\log T)$, and $\log\frac T{2\pi}-1=2a$ at $T=2\pi e^{2a+1}=e\Tst$.
\end{proof}

Below $e\Tst$ a function of type $a$ has more freedom than there are zeros to avoid; above it, fewer. So the minimizer can be expected to hide from the zeros up to about $e\Tst$ and to be the pure edge tail beyond, which is the onset of the tail law; and its floor to sit in the zeros around $e\Tst$. The data agree: the median height of the floor's mass is $2.39,\,2.32,\,2.65,\,2.67,\,2.74$ horizons at $a=0.6,\dots,1.5$ (Computation~\ref{comp:tail}), and $\vartheta(e\Tst)$ read from the same tables is $0.43,\,0.45,\,0.47,\,0.49,\,0.50$, while the law is within $10$--$15\%$ at that height. Conjecture~\ref{conj:A} then takes its sharpest conditional form.

\begin{proposition}[sampling above the exhaustion height implies Conjecture~\ref{conj:A}]\label{prop:sampling}
Assume RH, let $\kappa\ge1$, and suppose that for the minimizer $f$ at every support $a\ge a_0$
\begin{itemize}
\item[(S$_\kappa$)] $\displaystyle\int_{|t|>\kappa\Tst}|F(t)|^2\rho(t)\,dt\;\le\;M\sum_{|\gamma|>\kappa\Tst}|F(\gamma)|^2$ \quad (the zeros above $\kappa\Tst$ sample the tail of $F$ with constant $M$), and
\item[(E$_\kappa$)] $\displaystyle\int_{|t|>\kappa\Tst}|F(t)|^2\rho(t)\,dt\;\ge\;\frac{2C(a)^2}{\pi\kappa\Tst\,M'}$ \quad (the edge overlap of Lemma~\ref{lem:overlap} holds at height $\kappa\Tst$ within the factor $M'$),
\end{itemize}
and that the boundary law $\lambda'=-2C^2$ holds. Then $\lambda'\ge-\pi\kappa MM'\,\Tst\,\lambda$ on $[a_0,\infty)$: Conjecture~\ref{conj:A} with $c=\pi\kappa MM'$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
$\lambda=\sum_\gamma|F(\gamma)|^2\ge\sum_{|\gamma|>\kappa\Tst}|F(\gamma)|^2\ge M^{-1}\int_{|t|>\kappa\Tst}|F|^2\rho\ge\frac{2C^2}{\pi\kappa MM'\Tst}$, so $-\lambda'=2C^2\le\pi\kappa MM'\Tst\lambda$.
\end{proof}

Both hypotheses are measured. (S$_\kappa$) with $\kappa=e$: the density integrals and the zero sums of Computation~\ref{comp:tail} agree to $7\%$ at five horizons and to $1\%$ at twenty, so $M\approx1$; (E$_e$): $T\vartheta(T)/T_{\mathrm{eff}}$ at $e\Tst$ is $1.10$--$1.15$ at every support, so $M'\approx1$ with the inequality in the favourable direction. The measured constant, $c_0=\Phi'/\Tst$ between $3.3$ and $3.9$, is $\pi e\,\vartheta(e\Tst)$ times that factor, against the bound $\pi e\approx8.5$ of the proposition. What (S$_\kappa$) asks of the zeros is a sampling inequality on the half-line above $e\Tst$ for the high-frequency part of functions of type $a$ with a logarithmic edge, with density $\rho\ge(2a+1)/2\pi$ against the Nyquist density $a/\pi$; what (E$_\kappa$) asks of the minimizer is that its edge layer be no narrower than $1/(e\Tst)$, i.e.\ that it hide from no zero above the exhaustion height. The first is a statement about the zeros alone, the second about the minimizer alone, and the count of Lemma~\ref{lem:exhaust} is the reason both should hold at a fixed multiple of the horizon.

The entries have a zero-side reading in the same picture. The tail of the floor carries $\cos^2(\gamma a)=\tfrac12(1+\cos2\gamma a)$ over the zeros, so it contains the average of $\cos(2\gamma a)$ over the tail-weighted zeros, and by the explicit formula the zeros' rotations align exactly when $2a=\log n$.

\begin{computation}[the zeros' rotation at the entries]\label{comp:align}
\texttt{rh\_alignment.py}, first $6000$ zeros: $R(a)=\sum_\gamma\cos(2\gamma a)\,w(\gamma)/\sum_\gamma w(\gamma)$ with the tail weight $w=\gamma^{-2}\log^{-1}(\gamma/2\pi)$, on $0.3\le a\le1.3$ in steps of $0.0005$. Its mean absolute value is $0.27$ and its $95$th percentile $0.53$; at the entries of the primes it is $-0.53$ ($a_2$), $-0.62$ ($a_3$, the minimum over the whole range, at $a=0.5495$), $-0.57$ ($a_5$), $-0.56$ ($a_7$), $-0.44$ ($a_{11}$), each minimum within $0.0005$ of $a_n$; at the prime powers $4,8,9$, whose weights $\Lambda(n)n^{-1/2}=0.35,0.25,0.37$ are below the noise of this truncation, it is $+0.10,-0.08,+0.22$. Around $a_3$ the curve is a cusp: with the zeros above $3\Tst$ only and weight $\gamma^{-2}$, $R$ is $-0.097,-0.158,-0.226,-0.161,-0.103$ at offsets $-0.004,-0.002,0,+0.002,+0.004$, linear flanks meeting at the entry. A cusp in the tail mass is a jump in $\lambda'$: this is the kink of Proposition~\ref{prop:kink} seen from the zeros, and with the edge law in place of a jump it is the soft kink of \eqref{eq:soft}. The relay, the soft kink, the sawtooth of Computation~\ref{comp:relay} and the tail law are one mechanism: the zeros rotate with the window, align at the primes, and the alignment is felt through the tail that the edge feeds.
\end{computation}

The sampling hypothesis (S$_\kappa$) has an exact form: the explicit formula turns the defect of the zeros against their density into a prime sum, and the prime sum lives on the entries, where the autocorrelation of the minimizer is singular.

\begin{proposition}[the sampling defect is the prime sum at the entries]\label{prop:defect}
Assume RH. Let $f\in L^2(-a,a)$ be odd with $P=\ip fs$, and for $T>0$ let $\chi_T(t)=1-e^{-(t/T)^{2m}}$ with $m$ even ($m=2$ unless said otherwise), an entire even high-pass at height $T$, and
\[
\check h_T(x)=\frac1{2\pi}\int_\R|F(t)|^2\chi_T(t)e^{itx}\,dt ,
\]
the autocorrelation of $f$ with its structure below the scale $1/T$ removed. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:defect}
\sum_\gamma|F(\gamma)|^2\chi_T(\gamma)=\frac1{2\pi}\int_\R|F(t)|^2\chi_T(t)\Psi_\infty(t)\,dt-2\sum_{n\ge2}\Lambda(n)n^{-1/2}\,\check h_T(\log n)-2P^2\big(1-e^{-(2T)^{-2m}}\big),
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Weil's explicit formula in the normalization of \eqref{eq:Q} reads, for an even $h$ analytic in $|\Imm t|<\frac12+\varepsilon$ and $O(|t|^{-1-\varepsilon})$ there, $\sum_\gamma h(\gamma)=\frac1{2\pi}\int h\Psi_\infty-\sum_n\Lambda(n)n^{-1/2}\big(\check h(\log n)+\check h(-\log n)\big)+h(i/2)+h(-i/2)$, $\check h$ the inverse transform \cite{weil,bombieri}; for $h=|F|^2$ it is the identity $Q(f)=\sum_\gamma|F(\gamma)|^2$ of \S\ref{sec:rh}. Apply it to $h(t)=F(t)F(-t)\chi_T(t)$, which is entire and even, equals $|F|^2\chi_T$ on the line, and is $O(|t|^{-1-\varepsilon})$ in every strip, $f$ having compact support and $\chi_T$ being bounded there (for fixed $m$, $\mathrm{Re}\,(t/T)^{2m}>0$ in a strip once $|\mathrm{Re}\,t|\gg m$). For odd $f$, $F(\pm i/2)=\mp P$, so $h(\pm i/2)=-P^2\chi_T(i/2)=-P^2(1-e^{-(2T)^{-2m}})$, $i^{2m}=1$ for even $m$, and $\check h$ is even.
\end{proof}

The first term on the right of \eqref{eq:defect} is the density integral of (S$_\kappa$) with the exact density $\Psi_\infty/2\pi$, and the last is negligible. So the sampling constant is $M_\chi(T)=1+\big(2\sum_n\Lambda(n)n^{-1/2}\check h_T(\log n)+O(P^2(2T)^{-2m})\big)\big/\sum_\gamma|F(\gamma)|^2\chi_T(\gamma)$: the zeros above a height sample the minimizer at their mean density up to the prime sum at the entries. For an entry $m$ outside the window, $\check h_T(\log m)=(g*\check\chi_T)(\log m)$ is the autocorrelation smeared at scale $1/T$ to a point at distance $\log m-2a$ from its support, which is negligible once $T(\log m-2a)$ is large: this is the alignment of Computation~\ref{comp:align}, confined to a neighbourhood of width $1/T$ of each entry. For the entries inside the window the term is set by the echoes.

\begin{corollary}[the analytic form of the sampling constant]\label{cor:M}
Under RH and the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{prop:whfull} (one entry $n$ inside the window, $a_2<a<a_3$), with $c=\Lambda(n)n^{-1/2}$,
\begin{gather*}
M_\chi(T)=1+2c^2\,\big\langle\tilde\sigma_\infty^{-2}\big\rangle_T\big(1+o(1)\big)=1+\frac{2c^2}{\sigma_\infty(T)^2}\big(1+O(1/\log T)\big)\qquad(T\to\infty),\\
\langle g\rangle_T=\frac{\int_0^\infty\chi_Tg\,t^{-2}dt}{\int_0^\infty\chi_T\,t^{-2}dt} :
\end{gather*}
the defect of the sampling is twice the square of the echo's amplitude $\rho=c/\tilde\sigma_\infty$ of \eqref{eq:echo}, averaged over the tail with the weight $\chi_T/t^2$ of the edge, and tends to zero like $1/\log^2T$. The average matters at the heights computed: $\langle\tilde\sigma_\infty^{-2}\rangle_T=\sigma_\infty(T)^{-2}(1-2/\sigma_\infty(T)+\dots)$ is $0.7$ times $\sigma_\infty(T)^{-2}$ at $T=10\,\Tst$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
By Corollary~\ref{cor:full}(iii), $|F|^2=\frac{4|A_0|^2}{t^2\tilde\sigma}\big[\cos\varphi+\rho\cos(\varphi-t\log n)\big]^2(1+o(1))$ with $\varphi=ta+\theta-\kappa/t$. In $\check h_T(\log n)=\frac1{2\pi}\int|F|^2\chi_T\cos(t\log n)\,dt$ the cross term $2\rho\cos\varphi\cos(\varphi-t\log n)=\rho[\cos(t\log n)+\cos(2\varphi-t\log n)]$ gives, against $\cos(t\log n)$, the non-oscillating $\tfrac12\rho$, the other products oscillating at the frequencies $2a$, $2a\pm\log n$ and $2a\pm2\log n$, all nonzero; so $\check h_T(\log n)=\frac{|A_0|^2}{\pi}\int_0^\infty\frac{\rho\chi_T}{t^2\tilde\sigma}\,dt\,(1+o(1))$ with $\rho/\tilde\sigma=c/\tilde\sigma_\infty^2(1+O(\tilde\sigma_\infty^{-2}))$, while the density integral is $\frac1{2\pi}\int\frac{4|A_0|^2\cos^2\varphi}{t^2\tilde\sigma}\chi_T\Psi_\infty\,dt\,(1+o(1))=\frac{|A_0|^2}{\pi}\int_0^\infty\frac{\chi_T}{t^2}\,dt\,(1+o(1))$, which is $|A_0|^2I_4/(\pi T)$ with $I_4=\int_0^\infty(1-e^{-s^4})s^{-2}ds=\Gamma(3/4)$ for $m=2$. The entries outside the window and the polar term are $o$ of this, and \eqref{eq:defect} gives $M_\chi-1=2c\check h_T(\log n)/(\text{density integral})(1+o(1))=2c^2\langle\tilde\sigma_\infty^{-2}\rangle_T(1+o(1))$; the expansion of the average is $\int_1^\infty du/(u^2(L+\log u)^2)=L^{-2}(1-2/L+O(L^{-2}))$ with $L=\log(T/2\pi)-\lambda$, for a cutoff sharp at $T$.
\end{proof}

\begin{computation}[the identity on the zeros]\label{comp:defect}
\texttt{rh\_sampling\_defect.py}, first $6000$ zeros, logs in \texttt{data/}. On the minimizers with primes at $a=0.4,0.45,0.5$ (one entry inside the window) and $a=0.6$ (two entries, Computation~\ref{comp:tree06}), every term of \eqref{eq:defect} is evaluated at $T=\kappa\Tst$ for $\kappa=1,\dots,20$, with the cutoffs $m=2$ and $m=4$: the zero sum (with the part above the last zero, $305$--$455$ horizons, taken at the density), the density integral by quadrature on a grid of step $0.01$ to $3000$ and $0.05$ to $1.2\times10^5$, the prime sum over all $n<400$, and the polar term. The identity holds at every height to $3\times10^{-5}$ of the zero sum or better. The prime sum is the entries inside the window: at $a\le0.5$, $n=2$ gives all of it to three digits from $\kappa=e$ on, and $92$--$97\%$ at $\kappa=1$, the rest the smearing to $n=3$ at distance $0.1$--$0.3$ outside. The sampling constant $M_\chi=D_\chi/Z_\chi$ with the cutoff $1-e^{-(t/T)^8}$ against the averaged form $1+2c^2\langle\tilde\sigma_\infty^{-2}\rangle_T$ of Corollary~\ref{cor:M} and against $1+2c^2/\sigma_\infty(T)^2$:
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lccccccccc}
\toprule
$\kappa$ & \multicolumn{3}{c}{$a=0.4$} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{$a=0.45$} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{$a=0.5$}\\
 & $M_\chi$ & $\langle\cdot\rangle_T$ & at $T$ & $M_\chi$ & $\langle\cdot\rangle_T$ & at $T$ & $M_\chi$ & $\langle\cdot\rangle_T$ & at $T$\\
\midrule
$e$ & $1.068$ & $1.083$ & $1.151$ & $1.085$ & $1.076$ & $1.133$ & $1.128$ & $1.070$ & $1.120$\\
$6$ & $1.044$ & $1.047$ & $1.072$ & $1.046$ & $1.044$ & $1.066$ & $1.051$ & $1.041$ & $1.062$\\
$10$ & $1.034$ & $1.035$ & $1.050$ & $1.033$ & $1.033$ & $1.047$ & $1.032$ & $1.031$ & $1.044$\\
$20$ & $1.024$ & $1.024$ & $1.034$ & $1.023$ & $1.023$ & $1.032$ & $1.021$ & $1.022$ & $1.030$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
From six horizons on the defect is the echo's, averaged over the tail, to a few parts in a thousand, and the value at $T$ overestimates $M_\chi-1$ by a factor $1.4$: the average matters. Below six horizons the cutoff still reaches the heights where the minimizer hides from the zeros, the more so the smaller $\lambda$ ($\lambda=2\times10^{-4}$ at $a=0.5$), and $M_\chi$ carries that. With the quartic cutoff (log \texttt{sampling\_defect.log}) the leakage is larger and, between six and twenty horizons, happens to compensate the difference between the average and the value at $T$, so that $M_\chi$ there agrees with $1+2c^2/\sigma_\infty(T)^2$ to a few parts in a thousand; the sharper cutoff separates the two. The sharp constant $M(\kappa)$ of Computation~\ref{comp:analytic}, $1.01$, $1.09$, $1.02$ at $\kappa=e$ for the three supports, sees the same defect at the sharp edge of the tail.
\end{computation}

The arithmetic of Conjecture~\ref{conj:A} is now in one place. By \eqref{eq:defect} the sampling defect at every support is $2\sum_{\log n<2a}\Lambda(n)n^{-1/2}\check h_T(\log n)$ plus the alignment at the entries within $1/T$ of the window's edge, and (S$_\kappa$) with a bounded $M$ asks that this prime sum be at most a fixed fraction of the tail at $T=\kappa\Tst$, uniformly in $a$: a statement about the autocorrelation of the minimizer at the entries, that is, about the echoes, and no longer about the zeros. For one entry it is Corollary~\ref{cor:M}; for a window containing many entries the echoes form a finite tree and $\sum_{\log n<2a}\Lambda(n)^2/n\sim2a^2$ by Mertens, so the defect need not tend to zero, but it is bounded as long as the echoes keep their size $c_n/\sigma_\infty$. No gap condition on the zeros enters.

The two hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{prop:sampling} can be replaced by one, about the minimizer alone. By \eqref{eq:defect} with $\Psi=\Psi_\infty-\sum_n2\Lambda(n)n^{-1/2}\cos(t\log n)$, the zero sum with the cutoff is $\frac1{2\pi}\int|F|^2\chi_T\Psi$ minus the polar term: the prime sum at the entries is the oscillating part of the symbol. So what the zeros see is the tail of $|F|^2$ against the full symbol, and the sharp form of Computation~\ref{comp:envelope}, the local envelope of $t^2(\Psi-\lambda)|F|^2$, is the whole story.

\begin{proposition}[the sharp form as the single hypothesis]\label{prop:sharp}
Assume RH. Let $\kappa\ge1$, and for a support $a$ put $T=\kappa\Tst$ and $\chi_T^{(m)}(t)=1-e^{-(t/T)^{2m}}$ with $m$ the even integer in $[\tfrac32T-2,\tfrac32T)$. Suppose that for every support $a\ge a_0$
\begin{itemize}
\item[(T$_\kappa$)] $\displaystyle\frac1{2\pi}\int_\R|F(t)|^2\chi^{(m)}_T(t)\big(\Psi(t)-\lambda\big)\,dt\;\ge\;\frac{-\lambda'(a)}{\pi M''\,T}$ \quad (the sharp tail law holds at height $\kappa\Tst$ within the factor $M''$).
\end{itemize}
Then $\lambda'\ge-\pi\kappa M''\Tst\lambda-\varepsilon(a)$ on $[a_0,\infty)$ with $0\le\varepsilon(a)\le M''e^{-8\Tst(a)}$, and Conjecture~\ref{conj:A} holds with $c=\pi\kappa M''$ up to this term, which the Gr\"onwall argument of Section~\ref{sec:conj} absorbs.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For this $m$ the cutoff is bounded by $e^{1.01}$ in the strip $|\mathrm{Im}\,t|\le\tfrac12+\varepsilon$, $\varepsilon<0.02$: for $|\mathrm{Re}\,t|\le T$, $|(t/T)^{2m}|\le(1+(0.52/T)^2)^{3T/2}$, and for $|\mathrm{Re}\,t|\ge T$, $2m|\arg t|\le3(\tfrac12+\varepsilon)<\pi/2$, so $\mathrm{Re}\,(t/T)^{2m}>0$. Hence $h=F(t)F(-t)\chi_T^{(m)}$ satisfies the hypotheses of the explicit formula, and \eqref{eq:defect} holds with the polar term $2P^2\big(1-e^{-(2T)^{-2m}}\big)$, which lies in $[0,2P^2(2T)^{-2m}]$ since $m$ is even. Under RH, $\lambda=\sum_\gamma|F(\gamma)|^2\ge\sum_\gamma|F(\gamma)|^2\chi_T^{(m)}(\gamma)=\frac1{2\pi}\int|F|^2\chi_T^{(m)}(\Psi-\lambda)+\lambda\,\frac1{2\pi}\int|F|^2\chi^{(m)}_T-2P^2\big(1-e^{-(2T)^{-2m}}\big)$, and $\lambda\ge0$; so by (T$_\kappa$), $-\lambda'\le\pi M''T\lambda+2\pi M''TP^2(2T)^{-2m}$. The last term is $\varepsilon(a)$: $P^2\le\|s\|^2=\sinh a-a$, and $2m\log(2T)\ge(3\kappa\Tst-4)\log(2\kappa\Tst)$ gives $\varepsilon\le M''e^{-8\Tst}$ for $\Tst\ge2\pi e^{0.6}$, that is $a_0\ge0.3$. In the Gr\"onwall argument, $\lambda(a)e^{\frac c2\Tst(a)}\ge\lambda(a_0)e^{\frac c2\Tst(a_0)}-\int_{a_0}^a\varepsilon e^{\frac c2\Tst}$, and the integral is bounded by $M''\int e^{-(8-c/2)\Tst}$, negligible against the first term for $c<16$.
\end{proof}

Under the hypotheses of Corollary~\ref{cor:full} or~\ref{cor:tree}, (T$_\kappa$) holds at a fixed support for $\kappa$ large, with $M''\to1$: the sharp form is exact in the limit, and by Corollary~\ref{cor:tree}(iv) it is, in the finite tree, an identity of the echo amplitudes. What Conjecture~\ref{conj:A} asks is that the height from which the sharp form holds within a fixed factor be a bounded multiple of the horizon, uniformly in $a$. The measured onset is a few horizons at every support computed (Computations~\ref{comp:envelope}, \ref{comp:analytic}, \ref{comp:tail}: $0.1\%$ from five horizons on the prime-free critical points, a few per cent with the primes, including $a=1.0$ and $1.25$, where the echo set is infinite), and the bound $c=\pi e M''$ at $\kappa=e$ is again $8.5$ against the observed $3.3$--$3.9$. No sampling constant and no gap condition on the zeros remain in the hypothesis: the zeros enter only through the identity \eqref{eq:defect}.

\begin{heuristic}[prolate lower bound]\label{heur:prolate}
The number of zeros in $[-\Tst,\Tst]$ is $2N(\Tst)\approx(\Tst/\pi)(2a-1)$, short of the Shannon number $2a\Tst/\pi$ of $[-a,a]\times[-\Tst,\Tst]$ by $\Tst/\pi$. By the Landau--Pollak--Slepian theory \cite{slepian-pollak,lps}, with the asymptotics of the prolate spectrum in the plunge region \cite{slepian65,landau-widom} (about $(2/\pi^2)\log c$ eigenvalues between $\varepsilon$ and $1-\varepsilon$ for $c=a\Tst$), a function in $\PW_a$ constrained to vanish on all zeros below the horizon can still keep all but $\eta\gtrsim\exp(-\pi\Tst/(\log(a\Tst)+O(1)))$ of its energy below the horizon; under RH this bounds $\lambda$ from above, i.e.\ $\Phi\gtrsim\pi\Tst/\log(a\Tst)$, which is $0.7\,\Tst$ at $a=1$. The observed value is $\Phi(1)=59.5=1.28\,\Tst$ (Computation~\ref{comp:grid}); the zeros are about twice as expensive a constraint set as the count alone predicts, and the difference is arithmetic. Conjecture~\ref{conj:A} is the matching upper bound.
